Inter-Como verso il sold out: ultimi biglietti, live show e le info per i tifosi a San Siro

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Inter-Como verso il sold out: ultimi biglietti, live show e le info per i tifosi a San Siro

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

Dopo il successo in Champions League contro il Feyenoord, l'Inter ritrova subito San Siro: i nerazzurri scendono in campo contro il Como nell'11ª giornata di Serie A. Si gioca domenica 8 novembre alle 18:00.

La sfida del Meazza chiuderà un periodo ricco di impegni per i nerazzurri: Inter-Como, ultima partita prima della sosta del campionato, sarà un appuntamento affascinante, al quale i tifosi interisti parteciperanno con il consueto entusiasmo: la partita viaggia spedita verso il sold out. Gli ultimi biglietti disponibili per Inter-Como sono disponibili su inter.it/tickets grazie alla rivendita dedicata agli Abbonati FULL.

Domenica 8 novembre sarà in vigore la Determinazione dell’ONMS 40/2026, per la quale i residenti in provincia di Como avranno la possibilità di acquistare solo i biglietti del settore ospiti.

Il pre-partita di San Siro sarà ancora una volta ricco di intrattenimento e momenti imperdibili con l’Inter Live Show presentato da Daniele Battaglia e Mirko Mengozzi. Lo show accompagnerà i tifosi dall’apertura dei cancelli fino al fischio d’inizio: sul palco si alterneranno giochi, musica con il DJ e tante attività pensate per coinvolgere il pubblico per rendere unica l’attesa verso Inter-Como. Sarà inoltre attivo il live entertainment su maxischermo tramite QR code: una nuova possibilità per coinvolgere maggiormente i tifosi, rendendoli parte attiva dello spettacolo di San Siro. Il pubblico potrà scattarsi fotografie da trasmettere al maxischermo, essere coinvolto con divertenti fan cam, scegliere le tracce musicali preferite per caricare la squadra e partecipare a speciali quiz.

Come di consueto si terrà anche il concorso dedicato ai tifosi nerazzurri, con in palio le maglie gara dell’Inter. Partecipare è semplice e gratuito: sarà possibile farlo entro un’ora dal calcio d’inizio, con la premiazione che avverrà direttamente sul palco a bordocampo, regalando ai vincitori un’esperienza ancora più speciale nel cuore del Meazza.

Tra le tante attività previste nel pre partita di Inter-Como, i tifosi potranno iniziare a vivere le emozioni della gara attraverso lo speciale Light Show powered by Enel, match sponsor della sfida di San Siro. Uno spettacolo imperdibile, che permetterà al pubblico nerazzurro di entrare fin da subito nel clima partita partecipando in prima persona: i tifosi sono chiamati a diventare protagonisti, prendendo parte attraverso le torce dei propri smartphone allo speciale Light Show powered by Enel. Per farlo basterà inquadrare i QR code che saranno trasmessi sui maxischermi dello stadio: tutti i tifosi interessati potranno anche partecipare cliccando su questo link.

L'apertura dei cancelli per Inter-Como è in programma alle ore 16:00. Considerato il grande afflusso previsto si raccomanda a tutti i tifosi di presentarsi allo stadio a ridosso dell'orario di apertura dei cancelli.

Si ricorda a tutti i tifosi che è severamente vietato fumare all'interno dell'impianto. Inoltre è vietato introdurre i seguenti oggetti nello stadio: stampelle, ombrelli grandi, accendini, laser, fumogeni, bandiere o vessilli non precedentemente autorizzati, caschi, passeggini, trolley, apparecchi fotografici professionali, aste per selfie e computer portatili. In caso di pioggia sarà consentito l'ingresso solo agli ombrelli portatili e senza punta. In caso di mancato rispetto del regolamento, verrà applicato il codice di condotta.

La numerazione degli ingressi dello stadio è stata modificata: non sono più presenti gli ingressi dal 7 al 10. Sul lato ovest della cancellata, in corrispondenza dei settori rossi, non sono più previsti ingressi: queste aree saranno utilizzate esclusivamente come uscite di emergenza e potranno essere aperte durante il deflusso al termine della partita.

Le persone con disabilità potranno accedere allo stadio attraverso la PC4, situata a fianco dell'ingresso 11.

Agent Distraction

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

What is easy for humans to skim past in code, agents see as a call to action. They do not know better, latching on to that which is littered across the files read during the planning and research phase of the coding journey.

As agents' agency through tools and skills grows the ability for side-quests scales proportionally. Obsolete specs, long resolved bugs, and lagging documentation no longer rest in long forgotten corners of the codebase, but rather are unintentionally ingested as context indirecting and misdirecting the next wave of coders - the agents. This indirection is not free: in the best case it only costs tokens, steps, and time - in the worst case it distracts and grounds the agent on falsehoods leading to incorrect logic and decisions during implementation.

Measuring how often agents fall into these traps across millions of monthly sessions is impossible with manual human review - we need an objective signal derivable from the code itself. Inline TODO(bug/...) comments provide the perfect proxy. TODOs tend to be left behind long after the underlying bug is resolved or abandoned, while the linked bug ticket acts as the canonical record. By cross-referencing every TODO an agent encounters against the TODO's bug status and the user's prompt, we can automatically separate valid work from wasted detours at scale.

By leveraging the distributed traces of agent interactions across thousands of engineers and over hundreds of millions of individual agent steps, we quantify the impact of TODO distractions in the codebase and how agents act on them:

Controlling for developer, codebase complexity, task type, model tier, number of files read, initial prompt length, and the step where the bug reference appeared, using a multi-way fixed-effects regression with developer-clustered standard errors, we see that unprompted bug detours add substantial overhead (all stat sig p<0.0001). Per session:

The Obsolete Detour: In the following trajectory we can observe how this drift plays out. During an investigation into an unrelated bug The agent views SurfaceControlTest.java and sees // TODO(bug/XXX)

This bug, while fixed in 2023, remains lingering in the codebase leading to subsequent investigation distraction across Steps with the agent digging into the bug to learn more.

The Eager Agent: In the trajectory the agent encountered a TODO in the bug it was investigating and decided (unprompted) to "fix" the code

The agent executed multiple Code Search and internal knowledge search queries for the bug ultimately resulting in the agent changing the ModelDownloaderV1 code. Later in the trajectory the human challenged the scope creep.

With the model when admitting it was an incorrect increase in scope:

While in both of these cases they were only trivial to minor detours - they were real distractions costing time, tokens, and human intervention.

As part of addressing this finding at scale we created agents that scrubbed the totality of our codebases, finding bug references in the code that were marked as fixed or obsolete for cleanup. That step alone however was not enough to determine if the TODOs in the codebase were load-bearing or not. The agents had to go further looking at the history of the bug, the file, and changes to the codebase, at times spanning years of history. Only in cases where the agent was able to confirm that the TODO and the intent behind the TODO was obsolete did our agent send a change for review to delete it from the codebase. As a result of this multi-layer approach, our todo cleaner has been able to remove thousands of actually obsolete references over the last 6 months exceeding 95% acceptance from human reviewers.

In recap, as we task agents on more and more ambitious problems, "agent friendly codebases" require more than just the code in the hotpath being healthy. The forgotten corners of the codebase now more than ever carry a significant tax levied against agents.

Tooling like presubmit analyzers, entire corpus scanners, and automated coding agents to remediate dead-todos offer out of the box ready to scale solutions to address the tax enabling a healthier and distraction free codebase for agents to thrive.

Projects that once were on the backburner to cleanup lingering debt but deprioritized due to perceived low value need to be reevaluated with the new agents acting at scale on your codebase.

AI Overviews: Why Brand Mentions Matter More Than Backlinks

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at blog.geosearchleads.com

Backlinks remain an important part of the web's discovery and authority ecosystem, but they may not be the strongest off-site signal associated with a brand appearing in Google AI Overviews. An Ahrefs analysis of 75,000 brands found that branded web mentions had a substantially stronger correlation with AI Overview visibility than raw backlink counts.

The finding does not mean links no longer matter, nor does it establish that mentions cause AI citations. It does, however, give brands a clearer indication of what may distinguish companies that are repeatedly surfaced in AI-generated answers: being discussed, described and corroborated across the web.

According to Ahrefs' analysis of AI Overview brand visibility factors, branded web mentions correlated with AI Overview mentions at 0.664. Raw backlink counts showed a correlation of 0.218. In practical terms, the association for web mentions was roughly three times stronger in this dataset.

Ahrefs compared several off-site brand signals with the number of AI Overview mentions observed for the brands in its dataset. Branded web mentions were the strongest reported factor among those highlighted. Branded anchor text and branded search volume also showed stronger relationships than raw backlink totals.

The paid-brand metrics were weakly correlated with AI Overview mentions, at 0.216 for branded ad traffic and 0.215 for branded ad cost. That is a useful distinction for teams evaluating whether paid brand activity can substitute for broader recognition. The analysis suggests that ad spend alone is not reliably associated with AI Overview inclusion.

The distribution in the data was also uneven. About 26% of brands recorded no AI Overview mentions. Meanwhile, brands in the top quartile for web mentions averaged 169 AI Overview mentions, compared with 14 for the next tier. That gap points to a concentrated visibility pattern in which widely discussed brands can have a disproportionate presence.

A backlink can indicate that one page references another, but its meaning varies widely. Links may be navigational, promotional, syndicated or only loosely related to the subject a user asks about. A branded mention, especially in a relevant editorial context, can more directly connect a company to a category, product type, problem or expertise.

That distinction matters for generative search. AI-generated answers need to synthesize claims from material they can identify and compare. A brand that appears in independent reviews, editorial comparisons, specialist publications and relevant community discussions leaves more evidence that it is associated with a particular topic.

This is not an argument to abandon link earning. High-quality editorial coverage can produce both a citation-worthy mention and a valuable link. The practical change is to stop treating the link itself as the sole outcome. Teams should assess whether coverage accurately explains what the brand does, where it fits, and which claims can be independently verified.

Third-party recognition is only one side of the equation. A brand still needs clear source material on its own site. If product pages, service pages and documentation are vague, inconsistent or difficult to parse, external mentions cannot fully solve the problem.

Start by reviewing whether important pages provide direct, supportable answers to the questions a buyer or researcher would ask. Useful content often includes clearly stated capabilities, specifications, limitations, definitions, pricing or process information where applicable, and evidence behind material claims.

The goal is not to create pages solely for an AI system. It is to make first-party information specific enough for people, search engines and answer systems to understand. Consistent terminology across pages also helps reinforce the entities and topics a business wants to be associated with.

Traditional digital PR is often evaluated by placements and referring domains. Those remain useful measures, but the Ahrefs findings suggest the brief should include a second question: will this coverage establish a meaningful association between the brand and a relevant topic?

Original research, proprietary data, expert commentary and useful tools can give publishers a concrete reason to mention a company in context. A generic announcement may generate a short-lived burst of attention, while a credible data point or distinctive expert perspective can be cited in coverage that explains a category.

When planning outreach, consider whether the anticipated article is likely to:

This approach should not become an attempt to manufacture mentions. Repetitive, low-quality placements may add noise without building the trust signals that independent coverage is meant to provide.

The strongest off-site footprint is not limited to press coverage. Relevant independent reviews, editorial comparisons and niche discussions can help demonstrate that a brand is recognized beyond its own marketing materials.

For ecommerce businesses, that may mean reputable product reviews and comparisons that accurately describe the offering. For software companies, it may include analyst-style coverage, practitioner reviews or discussions in specialist communities. For agencies and professional services firms, expert commentary, case-based editorial coverage and credible industry citations may be more relevant.

Quality and relevance matter more than a simple mention total. The research reports correlations, not a formula for earning AI Overview inclusion. A large number of irrelevant mentions should not be treated as an equivalent substitute for trusted, topically aligned references.

A useful audit combines first-party clarity with an assessment of how the wider web describes the brand.

Map priority topics and questions. Identify the products, services, use cases and category questions for which AI Overview visibility would be meaningful. Check whether your site has a clear, factual page supporting each topic.

Review third-party topic associations. Collect relevant editorial coverage, reviews, comparisons and specialist discussions. Look for whether they name the brand, describe it accurately and connect it to the intended category or use case.

Separate mentions from links in reporting. Track referring domains and backlinks, but also monitor unlinked mentions, branded anchors and the context of coverage. A link report alone may miss the signals most closely associated with AI Overview visibility in the Ahrefs dataset.

Monitor the answers themselves. Search results can vary by query and change over time. Record whether relevant AI Overviews appear for priority queries, which brands are cited or mentioned, and whether the underlying sources reveal content gaps or third-party validation opportunities.

For teams adapting to AI-led discovery, create a GEO Search Leads account to begin assessing your website's GEO readiness and identify optimization opportunities around the content and brand signals that support clearer AI-search visibility. The most useful next step is not chasing a single metric, but building a repeatable view of where your site explains key topics well and where independent web evidence is still limited.

Do backlinks still matter for AI Overview visibility?

Yes, backlinks can still support search visibility and discovery. However, Ahrefs' analysis found raw backlink counts had a weaker correlation with AI Overview mentions, at 0.218, than branded web mentions, branded anchors and branded search volume.

Do brand mentions cause a company to appear in AI Overviews?

No. The Ahrefs findings show correlation, not causation. Brand mentions may be associated with other factors, such as genuine market recognition, strong content or broader editorial coverage.

Can paid search activity improve AI Overview visibility?

The analysis found weak correlations for branded ad traffic, at 0.216, and branded ad cost, at 0.215. The data does not support treating paid brand activity as a reliable route to AI Overview mentions.

What types of third-party coverage are most useful?

Relevant independent coverage is likely more useful than generic publicity. Examples discussed in the research include reviews, editorial comparisons and niche discussions that accurately corroborate a brand's capabilities or topical relevance.

The Ahrefs study shifts the AI Overview conversation beyond link volume. Brands seeking stronger visibility should continue earning quality links, but pair that work with extractable first-party facts and credible third-party evidence that consistently associates them with the topics they want to be known for.

Looking a word up, and looking a claim up

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking a word up, and looking a claim up

Two things I do every week, and they look like the same activity right up until the moment you try to automate them.

In one case I have a word and want to know what it means. In the other I have a claim and want to know whether it holds up. The first is a lookup. The second is a comparison, and it needs a different kind of reference.

Knowing a word and not knowing it are different problems, and the second one is not solved by a dictionary.

A dictionary tells you the accepted meaning. What you usually need is narrower: what the word means in the specific community you are in, where it came from, and whether using it sounds right or sounds like a joke.

Taboords is that kind of reference: definitions of gírias, siglas, relationship terms, psychology, subcultures and Japanese culture, with the origin and the register next to each entry, at https://taboords.com.

The origin is the part that earns the lookup. A word with a documented origin is a word you can use with confidence, because you know the context it came from.

The other case is harder to recognise because it feels like reading. Something tells you a supplement works, or that a training method is better, or that a number means something, and you want to check it before you spend money on it.

The problem is that marketing copy and evidence look identical when you read them in a hurry. The claim is always confident. The difference is whether anybody lined several of them up against the same criteria and published the result.

Treino is that kind of base: supplement comparisons, exercise references and calculators, arranged for practical choices rather than for browsing, at https://treinando.org.

There is one practical way to tell the two apart, and it is about how you leave the page.

After a word lookup you leave with a word. After a claim lookup you should leave with a number and a reason, because the useful output is not the verdict but the criteria that produced it.

Neither of these is a substitute for thinking. But a reference that gives you the criteria is doing half the work for you, and that is most of what people actually need from a lookup site.

Underkeep is my favorite old-school dungeon crawler since Legend of Grimrock, and yes that includes Queen's Domain

Queen's Domain might be the trendy game of the moment but for my money, the classic grid-based action of Underkeep is where it's at. (And yes, there's a demo.)

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Everyone's talking about Queen's Domain right now, but as someone who tried Queen's Domain and did not care for it, allow me to direct your attention to an alternative old-school dungeon crawler: Underkeep, which launched on Steam on November 5 and has been absolutely phenomenal through the roughly four hours I've put into it so far.

Like Queen's Domain, Underkeep is firmly rooted in the 1990s, but I'm talkin' the early '90s, and even before that: Classic grid-based RPGs like The Bard's Tale, Dungeon Master, Wizardry, and Eye of the Beholder. In fact, as someone who loves Eye of the Beholder (specifically the second one, The Legend of Darkmoon), I noticed a lot of nearly direct lifts from that series: primarily visual, but also a few audio cues, that I swear I've heard before.

But my concerns that Underkeep might be a low-effort knockoff evaporated pretty quickly. After four hours of play I feel like I've barely scratched the game's surface: I still haven't found the titular village of Underkeep, where I assume the real action will begin, but I've crashed (literally, in a few cases) through multiple dungeons, all interconnected horizontally or vertically, and explored an overworld that's a whole lot bigger than I expected. At the very least, Underkeep looks beefy.

Underkeep's gameplay preserves the vibe of those old-time RPGs while eliminating most of their more objectionable nonsense. Its shared inventory is divided into categories that make managing and swapping equipment between party members extremely easy, the automapping is detailed while still allowing freehand notes (I know, I know, get some paper and do your own maps, but who has time for that these days?), and tools are used automatically without needing to be equipped.

And unlike so many games in the genre, Underkeep is entirely turn-based. This eliminates the cheese strategy of hitting an enemy and moving a step away before they can hit back, which carried so many gamers through so many fights in so many games over the years. But it also encourages more thoughtfulness: When I see an enemy in the distance, I can take my time to think about what I want to do, move to a more advantageous position, or even just go take a break for a while.

I'm free to do whatever I want until melee combat actually begins. Once that happens, everyone in the party has to finish their turn, and then let the enemies finish theirs, at which point we're all free to run away (and probably be chased) or launch another round of fighting. I'll always come down on the real-time-with-pause side of the argument for isometric RPGs, but the turn-based approach is ideal for Underkeep.

Character classes are a fairly standard mix—fighter, paladin, wizard, thief, that sort of thing—but the races are a bit more interesting. Humans and dwarves are on the menu, and "elk" are kind of like elves, but then you get the stoneborn, raven, and slugman, all of them nicely weird. Stoneborn, for instance, are basically golems⁠—extremely strong and tough, but they have the agility of a Mack truck and can't wear body armor. They're basically a boulder that can hit you really hard, which is great in some ways but has some real drawbacks too. Ravens, meanwhile, are subject to random mutations that can take them off in unforeseeable directions: My own raven character recently mutated a healing buff called Roommate from the Void: "Something moved into your body at some point and never left. It pays rent in blood."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Slugmen, well, they're just slugs who are also men. They look like this:

There's also no limit to combining races and classes: A stoneborn thief or slugman paladin may not be a great idea, but if you want to give it a shot, knock yourself out.

There's not a lot of reading to do in Underkeep, but the writing for those encounters is pleasingly evocative in an old-school tabletop way, and there are plenty of surprises to be had too. Depending on how you deal with the red-cloaked guy above, for instance, you may get a reward, you might get jumped, or you could find yourself facing some sort of bigger trouble down the road—that's not confirmed yet, but I definitely got some 'Be seein' you' stinkeye from him before he slunk off into the next life.

It makes me a little sad to say, but one of my first thoughts when I saw Underkeep was whether the assets were made with generative AI. I'm not the only one to have that worry: Multiple arguments about it are underway in the Steam forums right now. Unlike some people in those conversations who say they "can just tell" when AI is present, I cannot—but the Steam store page has no AI disclosure, and developer Rake in Grass has repeatedly insisted that no generative AI was used.

"All artwork in Underkeep was hand-drawn by our artists," an FAQ states. "Some variations in style are natural, as several artists contributed to the game. The occasional "mixels" are caused by dynamic scaling used to simulate 3D perspective and smooth animations."

It's unfortunate that debates like this are so commonplace now, because Underkeep is a joy: I haven't played a whole huge lot of them in recent years, but this is easily my favorite grid-based RPG since the great Legend of Grimrock 2, which will soon be 15 years old.

Underkeep is out now on Steam for $19.50, a 15% launch discount on the regular price that's available until November 19. You can also sample the goods by way of the demo, which offers about an hour of highly compressed gameplay and some fun surprises.

Two kinds of show me another one

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There is a specific moment in an online purchase where the page stops being useful, and it is rarely the moment the price changes.

It is the moment you look at the listing and realise it does not say what you needed it to say. So you go looking for the same thing elsewhere, and the search becomes a second project.

Two small tools exist for that, and they solve the problem in opposite directions.

One of them starts from the ad you already have. You paste the product link and it puts the alternatives next to it, so the comparison happens without you leaving the page.

Outros.link does exactly that: the item you were looking at, plus a handful of similar ones, so you can compare and then go back to the shop. It is at https://outros.link.

The useful part is that the starting point is a real listing, not a search term. Search engines optimise for what people type; this starts from what you already decided you want.

The other one starts from the job instead of the product.

Some things you need are not products, they are people: somebody who does the repair, who makes the thing, who comes to the place. The hard part is not finding the service, it is finding out whether the service exists near you and whether it answers the phone.

Fechando is a directory of trades with phone numbers and no account required, at https://fechando.com.

It sounds too simple to be useful, and then it turns out to save twenty minutes every time, which over a year is a lot of twenty minutes.

One of them assumes you already know what you want and need a comparison. The other assumes you do not yet know who can do the work for you.

Knowing which question you are actually asking saves the twenty-minute detour of searching for the wrong thing. Most of the time I did not have a search problem at all. I had an information problem, and no amount of typing would have fixed it.

797 of 1,071 TikTok Shop Reviews Came With No Text At All. The 1-Star Ones Talk Twice as Often

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I run a visa agency in Bali. Scrapers on Apify are the other half of my week, and one of them does a narrow job: every buyer review of a TikTok Shop product in the US or the UK — stars, text, date, photos, the variant bought, the reviewer.

The reason people buy review data is text: the complaints, the feature requests, the sentence that explains why 4.5 stars is not 4.9. So before writing another word about "AI review analysis", I counted how much text is actually in there. On the product my own health check hits every night, the answer is a quarter of it.

The test product is a US supplement listing, 1729448464509734958 — Toplux Magnesium Complex. On 8 October TikTok reported 170,737 reviews on it; today it reports 180,293, so about 330 arrive a day.

Sample one: a single run on 8 October, reviewsNewerThan: "3 days", cap 5,000. It returned 1,071 reviews in 46 seconds, and reviewText was an empty string on 797 of them — 74.4%.

Sample two, in case that was one strange weekend: twelve nightly 100-row runs between 13 October and today, deduplicated by reviewId into 964 unique reviews. Empty text on 686 — 71.2%.

Together, 2,035 unique reviews, 1,483 of them with nothing written in them: 72.9%. The 274 texts in sample one add up to 3,087 words. That is a long blog post, extracted from 1,071 paid rows.

Nothing is broken: TikTok Shop asks for a star rating in one tap after delivery and treats the text box as optional. But if your pipeline is rows → LLM → themes, you are paying for four rows to get one sentence, and you should know that going in.

Here is the part that changes an analysis. Share of reviews carrying any text, by star rating:

An angry buyer is two to three times more likely to type something than a happy one. The middle of the scale is noisy because the counts are small, but both ends replicate across two samples a month apart.

The consequence is worth a sticky note. In sample one the mean rating over all 1,071 rows is 4.491; over the 274 rows that have text it is 4.190. Drop the silent rows — exactly what a text-mining pipeline does — and the product loses 0.3 stars before any model sees it.

So compute the distribution from every row, and read the text knowing it over-represents the 1-star tail by design. If you want complaints and nothing else, ask for them instead of filtering a full export: reviewsFilter: "1" walks the 1-star filter directly, and on a UK lash-extension product it returned all 26 one-star reviews in a single API call, 17 with text (65% — same pattern, other market).

And photos are rarer than the Store screenshots suggest: 55 of 1,071 rows have at least one image (5%), and 12 of those 55 have a photo and no text, which is the only case where reviewImages is the whole review.

The two that do work, with a catch each. reviewerId is a stable numeric ID — 987 distinct reviewers in sample one, 39 of them reviewing twice — but it is null on 47 rows (4.4%), so your group-by needs a fallback bucket. And reviewerName is masked by TikTok before it reaches me: J**, A**a, 💋**🧸. Sample one has 900 distinct masked names for 987 reviewers, and ** appears 8 times. Never deduplicate reviewers by name.

Timestamps, at least, are honest: reviewedAt is a real millisecond-precision UTC instant on all 1,071 rows, not a "4 months ago" string.

This is the limit to plan around. One sort order plus one filter stops at about 5,000 reviews: page 51 of 100 answers has_more: false. Asking deeper gets you nothing.

To go past it, the Actor reads the star breakdown out of the first answer and walks the combinations — each star filter, the other sort order for buckets over 5,000, the photos-only and verified-only filters — dropping duplicate reviewIds. That is how "25,000 reviews per product" is reached, and 25,000 is the hard cap per run.

On a product with 180,293 reviews, that is 14% at the absolute best, and it is not a random 14%. The honest ways to use this:

A smaller listing has no such problem: under 5,000 the first walk gets everything, and the run statistics report TikTok's own total per product so you can check.

POST /api/shop/pdp_desktop/get_product_reviews only answers a request signed by TikTok's own page scripts. Plain HTTP returns {"code":10000} on every route I tried — direct, residential proxy, a commercial unblocker, the mobile twin of the page — and headless Chromium is refused as well (code 100000). What works is a headful browser on a virtual display: open one product page of the market, wait for the security scripts, then ask the API for any product ID in that market. page_size: 100 is accepted — 1,071 reviews took 13 calls and one browser launch.

Two quirks. An empty page that arrives without has_more: false is a hiccup, not the end of the list — ask again and it comes back. And geography is per market: US reviews answer from Apify's own datacenter address, a UK product needs a UK residential one, and a UK product asked from a US page returns zero reviews.

Two stars, text in Spanish that reads like praise, one photo. That row contains most of this article.

From Python, run it and measure what I have been measuring:

When it is the wrong tool. Only US and UK products serve the review list on the web — a link from any other market gets a free notice row explaining that. If you need price, stock, sales or shop stats too, reviews alone will not give them to you; that is a job for TikTok Shop Scraper. There are no seller replies and no Q&A, because the public page has none. And if your deliverable is prose for a model to read, budget by words, not by rows: on this listing, 1,000 paid reviews buy about 2,900 words over 256 texts, 60 of them three words or fewer.

Where Claude Code, Codex and Cursor Keep Your Chat History on Disk, and How to Read It

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Claude Code chat history is stored on your own machine as plaintext JSONL files under ~/.claude/projects/, one file per session, and deleted after 30 days by default. On Windows the same folder is %USERPROFILE%\.claude\projects\. Codex and Cursor keep their own records in their own places and formats. Paths and defaults below were last verified on 6 November 2026, and they do change, so check them against your installed version.

Most of us never open these files. We should, for two reasons. They record real work: why you dropped Redis for SQLite, the three-hour bug behind a one-line fix, the reason the repo always uses pnpm. Yet you may still re-explain your project every morning while all of that sits on disk.

They also hold more than you'd guess. Claude Code's own docs (the "Explore the .claude directory" page) say anything that passes through a tool lands in the transcript: file contents, command output, pasted text. That makes these folders worth knowing about whether you want to mine them or clean them out.

Each session is a file at ~/.claude/projects//.jsonl. The project folder name is your working directory with separators swapped for dashes, such as -Users-you-my-project. Each transcript holds messages, tool calls and tool results, plus the system prompt and tool definitions Claude Code sent the model.

Typed prompts: these live separately in ~/.claude/history.jsonl, which powers up-arrow recall. The 30-day sweep doesn't normally touch that file, so it stays until you remove it.

Retention: the cleanupPeriodDays setting controls the age limit. The default is 30 and the minimum is 1. Transcripts from sessions last used in Claude Desktop or Cowork follow a separate rule and are kept at any age by default.

Moving it: set CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR and all of this moves with it.

Codex is less documented here. OpenAI's config reference covers settings, but community write-ups are the source for the layout: ~/.codex/sessions/YYYY/MM/DD/rollout--.jsonl. One of those is "Where Six AI Coding CLIs Store Your Session Logs" (Adrian Ghinda's All About Coding, August 2026), which calls the path undocumented. Treat it as observed behaviour. Another community post reports Zstandard-compressed .jsonl.zst rollouts, so run ls before you assume.

There's also ~/.codex/history.jsonl. OpenAI's reference says history.persistence controls whether transcripts are saved there. The Ghinda write-up found it holds only the text you typed, and that setting it to none leaves the rollout files running. Sources disagree, so test it on your version if it matters to you. The same write-up found no documented age-based deletion, which means rollouts pile up until you remove them. CODEX_HOME moves the whole tree.

Cursor's data-use page doesn't say where chats live. Community tooling points to SQLite databases named state.vscdb, under the User folder:

macOS: ~/Library/Application Support/Cursor/User/

Look in globalStorage/state.vscdb and in workspaceStorage//state.vscdb. Write-ups disagree on which holds the chat content, so check both. The Cursor CLI is separate: the Ghinda write-up places its chats at ~/.cursor/chats///store.db. None of this is a stable interface, and an update can rearrange it.

You don't need a special viewer. JSONL is one JSON object per line, so grep and jq go a long way. To find every Claude Code session that mentions a decision:

To read just what you typed in one session (this skips messages stored as content arrays):

Codex works the same way against ~/.codex/sessions/. Field names differ and neither format is a promised API, so look at a line or two first.

For Cursor, use sqlite3 in read-only mode. A copy of the file is safer still:

Run one more search while you're here, for credentials in common shapes:

A clean result only means none of those patterns matched. It doesn't prove the folder is free of secrets.

For deletion, Claude Code has the best tool. claude purge ~/work/my-repo --dry-run prints exactly what would go, and dropping the flag deletes it after a confirmation. Versions before v2.1.288 call it claude project purge. For Codex, the Ghinda write-up says v0.136 and later ship a delete subcommand. Removing a rollout file by hand also works. For Cursor, delete chats inside the app. If you must touch the database, close Cursor and back up the whole User folder first.

Before you wipe ~/.claude/projects/, know that it also holds auto memory. Claude Code's docs say deleting the folder costs you resume, continue and rewind for past sessions, plus auto memory for every project.

Grep answers "did I ever say this?" It's poor at "what should my agent know right now?" Three things get in the way.

Tool boundaries. A decision made in Claude Code sits in a JSONL file that Cursor will never open. Cursor's reasoning sits in a SQLite blob that Codex can't see. If you work across several agents, you have several partial histories and no shared one. A second laptop has its own set again.

Volume. Transcripts carry tool output, system prompts and dead ends. Pasting them back into a chat costs far more tokens than they're worth, and that spend comes straight off your Claude, Cursor or Codex limits. We've written about cutting those token costs in more detail.

No sense of what still matters. A log treats a reversed decision and a current one the same. Last month's "use Redis" looks as true as this week's "we moved to SQLite." And Claude Code deletes the record at 30 days whether the content still matters or not.

At Open Agentic Brain & Memory (OpenAB), we think these files are the best raw material you already have, and that they should stay yours. We read the same transcripts and local stores described above, then distill decisions, fixes and preferences into memories tagged by project. They go into a local SQLite database, with no account and no server of ours. Sync between machines is an age-encrypted file in your own private GitHub repo, and you can read, edit, pin or delete every memory in a local control panel.

The installer asks one question that matters here: start from the past sessions your AI tools already keep on this computer, or start fresh. Choose the first and Claude Code, Codex and Cursor history that's still on disk becomes the starting memory, so you begin with your own work behind you. You can change your mind later with brain past-sessions on or off. Since Claude Code only keeps 30 days by default, what you have today may be more than you have next month.

After that, each tool gets a briefing of at most 2,000 tokens at session start, in 84 ms (p50), and can recall on demand over MCP in 28 ms (p50) across 10,000 memories. In our own benchmarks, loading context for a 10,000-memory store takes 1,325 tokens instead of 1,131,367. On LoCoMo we keep 97% of the full-conversation score while reading 18% of the tokens. Fewer tokens per session means you hit your limits later.

One honest limit: OpenAB runs on macOS and Linux, and Windows isn't supported yet, so the Windows paths above are for your own digging. You can see the benchmarks and the install steps on the OpenAB home page.

Install Open Agentic Brain & Memory (OpenAB)

Yes, the tools keep running, but you lose features tied to that history. Deleting ~/.claude/projects/ removes resume, rewind and auto memory, while ~/.claude/settings.json and ~/.claude.json should stay. Codex rollouts can go, though you can't resume those sessions afterwards. Leave Cursor's state.vscdb alone and delete chats in the app, because that database holds other editor state too.

They can. Claude Code's docs confirm that transcripts aren't encrypted at rest, that OS file permissions are the only protection, and that a .env file a tool reads ends up in the transcript. Assume the same risk for Codex and Cursor. Run the grep above and rotate anything it finds. Our self-hosted memory privacy guide covers how OpenAB handles this.

Both. The files are local, but your prompts and the model's replies go to the provider so the model can answer. Claude Code's "Data usage" page lists Anthropic's server-side retention at 30 days for commercial accounts and for consumers who opt out of model training, and 5 years for consumers who opt in. Cursor's data-use page says requests pass through its backend even with your own API key, and Privacy Mode changes what providers may keep. For Codex, read OpenAI's data controls for your plan.

Set cleanupPeriodDays to a higher number in ~/.claude/settings.json. Don't try 0: it fails validation. Longer retention also means sensitive content sits in plaintext for longer.

Claude Code has no undo for this, and claude purge warns that it can't be undone. Your only route is a backup, such as Time Machine or a filesystem snapshot taken before the deletion.

Use grep -rl across ~/.claude/projects/ to find the file, then jq to read it. Inside Claude Code, Ctrl+R searches your typed prompts.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio review: Great specs. Shame about the price, though

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses attempt to curb the controversy by removing the cameras, and while they’re still an excellent product, the price makes them harder to recommend.

This has been a bit of a rollercoaster year for Meta wearables. Meta’s VR division shrank considerably earlier this year, then later announced a massively redesigned follow-up that’ll launch next year. And while Meta has debuted dozens of new smart glasses styles throughout the year that have sold quite well, the company can’t seem to get away from controversy. That’s where Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses come in.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses do exactly what they sound like. It’s a pair of smart glasses powered by Meta AI and only includes the audio part of the Ray-Ban Meta experience. There are exactly zero cameras on these glasses, and the hope (obviously) is that they’ll provide a smart experience without the potential privacy problems. The problem, for me at least, is that they’re simply too expensive despite lacking standout features.

Now, that doesn’t mean Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses aren’t worthwhile. They feature a much wider prescription range (-12 to +6) than the majority of Meta smart glasses models, which often cap out at -6 to +6. The battery life is also significantly better thanks to the lack of a camera and other improvements in the hardware. And, of course, if you want a pair of AI glasses but don’t want the camera, these are the only options from Meta.

If I were to choose between the two available models, I’d pick the Clubmaster style every time. Not only do they look better overall and feature the thinnest frames of any Meta smart glasses, but they also have substantially better nosepads that are adjustable and can be easily replaced.

The Burbank model looks just fine, but the nosepads aren’t the same, and the glasses feel more rigid overall (which I find less comfortable). Both models come in lots of colors with many different lens options, including clear, tinted, polarized, Transitions, and prescription.

Both models feature a touchpad on the outside of the right temple arm, as well as a small, circular button up top to activate Meta AI. You can alternatively use the “Hey, Meta” keywords to activate Meta AI. The button and touchpad can be disabled if you like. Only the touchpad has an alternative function, and that alternative function only controls music playback via Spotify or Amazon Music. Other Meta glasses offer far more options.

Both styles are the lightest Meta glasses available. Most Meta smart glasses with cameras weigh in around the 54g mark. Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses weigh just 44g. They’re not as light as Even Realities G2, RayNeo iO, or MemoMind One, but 44g is still considered pretty light for smart glasses.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses reuse the case from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2, which looks more like a traditional Ray-Ban case rather than the foldable one that ships with Meta Ray-Ban Display or the 2026 Meta Camera Glasses. I don’t have an issue with Ray-Ban Meta Audio’s case at all, but I really enjoy the foldable one and wish it were included.

Audio playback on these is great, and while the speakers are identical to all other Meta glasses products, using them as spatial surround sound “headphones” while watching a movie with surround sound is a fantastic experience. They also have spatial audio recording and truly excellent noise cancellation thanks to the 6-mic setup.

But the lack of the newer processor from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 3 means the glasses don’t support the new hearing aid function. To me, that’s a massive missed opportunity since these are audio-only glasses. When I asked Meta about it, they only said it wasn’t available on Ray-Ban Meta Audio for now, so this problem could be fixed in an upcoming update.

And that’s actually where Meta’s glasses might shine brightest: software updates. The company has been exemplary with update cadence on all of its products, and its smart glasses constantly get new, innovative features that surprise and delight. While I’m a little underwhelmed with the number of features available on these glasses today, this product will likely look quite different a year from now, a fact that’s backed up by years of Meta significantly improving product feature sets over time.

If you buy these glasses today, know that you’re mainly investing in them for the future. They’re fine enough for today’s usage, especially if you’re interested in a hands-free way to use Meta AI products like Muse or just want an open-ear way to listen to music and take phone calls, but Meta’s penchant for continually adding new features is, undoubtedly, going to be the highlight for everyone who ever buys them.

Someone Asked Timothèe Chalamet How He Feels About Dune’s Ending (And Now I Can't Wait)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

My Son And Students Have No Desire To See Avengers: Doomsday. Here's Why I Think That's Very Telling

I Can't Get Over Dune 3's Popcorn Bucket Having Two Holes (And Neither Can Timothèe Chalamet)

I’m Loving This Adorably Awkward Clip Zendaya And Co. Dropped To Celebrate The Dune Movies Returning To Theaters

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Like millions of other people, I’ve been eagerly anticipating Dune: Part Three, which is going to hit the 2026 movie schedule on December 18. The book-to-screen adaptation will serve as the third and final installment of director Denis Villeneuve’s trilogy that started way back during COVID, if you can believe it. The movie stars Timothèe Chalamet, of course, as Paul Atreides, just like the first two movies, and recently, Chalamet and his castmates sat down for an interview. He was asked about the ending of the movie, and his answer has me even more excited.

As with any source material as beloved as the Dune series of books, fans are usually nervous about an adaptation sticking the landing, and that’s certainly true of Dune: Part Three. Fans of the series know how the book ends, but will the movie change it? Improve on it? Screw it up? Chalamet was asked by one of his co-stars, Jason Momoa, in an interview with the cast in The New York Times about the ending, and Chalamet’s answer is ambiguous, but definite:

Absolutely. It didn’t feel like the bar dropped at all, Denis just changed the location of the bar. This movie is its own thing.

Now, calling the movie “its own thing” might make some book fans nervous, but that comes as a response from something another co-star, Robert Pattinson. It brings a little more context to what Chalamet said. Pattinson said:

The script was really cool, but it was quite complicated. And watching the film, it’s not about what I thought it was going to be about at all. It reads as much more of a grand political, moral fable, and the movie is so much more emotional.

This all stemmed from a question from The Times asking if the movie departs from the source material, the second book in the series, Dune Messiah. As a fan of the books (although I admittedly haven’t read Messiah in quite a few years), I welcome changes. I don’t want to stray too far, of course, but books and movies are very different mediums, and changes need to be made. The important thing is that the message of the book is translated to the big screen, and it sounds like, at least according to Chalamet, that Villeneuve’s done that.

Pattison calling it a political fable is exactly what I want, as that was ultimately the purpose of the author, Frank Herbert. The Dune series isn’t just a sci-fi story set far into the future, it’s meant to be a morality tale for us, here on Earth today (even if Messiah is almost 60 years old now). Ultimately, any concerns I had were put to rest as I was reading the interview when yet another A-list co-star, Anya Taylor-Joy, reminded us who is in charge here:

Denis is literally the biggest Dune fan of all time, so for that to be the person who’s steering the ship, you feel pretty good about it.

Feel good, I do. I think this is going to be epic in every way. In the meantime, now is a good time to fire up your HBO Max subscription to watch the first two Dune movies.

Kourtney Kardashian Slams ‘Fabricated’ Rumors That She Doesn’t Live With Travis Barker and Shares ‘Really Vulnerable’ Renovation of Calabasas Home

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Kourtney Kardashian is setting the record straight on her living arrangements with her husband, Travis Barker.

On the Nov. 5 episode of "The Kardashians," the Poosh founder is seen driving around town with her close friend Simon Huck. She mentions that she received a phone call from her publicist, asking her to comment on previous speculation that Kardashian and the Blink-182 drummer were living separately and planning to split.

Kardashian called the claims "speculated rumors."

"I go, 'Tell them to print it. See what happens,'" she recalled.

"I was just like, 'They know it's not true. They know they saw a dumb TikTok of someone who makes up fabricated stories,'" Kardashian continued.

Huck agreed, noting he often sees "crazy, weird conspiracy theories" about Kardashian and her famous family.

"People believe it," Kardashian said of the gossip, which is sometimes fueled by AI-generated clips. It is "so bizarre."

Moments later, Kardashian, Huck, and her stepson, Landon Barker, pulled up to her longtime Calabasas home, which is undergoing a major renovation.

In the scene, Kardashian said she expected they were about six months away from being able to move back in. But she was beginning to second-guess that timeline after seeing how few changes had been made since her last visit.

"It's a really vulnerable feeling to have all of the walls of your home ripped apart. There's no electrical. There's nothing," she said in a confessional interview during the episode. "It's like a very vulnerable feeling."

While the renovation was in too early a stage to gather much about Kardashian's design plans, she did bring the cameras through a guest room that she said was going to be for Landon, before he moved into a place of his own.

The 23-year-old rapper was quick to pipe up and claim the space, however, saying that he'd love to have his own space because his current residence is a rental.

Barker and Kardashian have a large blended family of seven children. In November 2023, they welcomed a son, Rocky Thirteen Barker. Before their marriage in 2022, Kardashian had three children with her ex Scott Disick: Mason Disick, 16, Penelope Disick, 14, and Reign Disick, 11.

In addition to Landon, Barker and his ex Shanna Moakler are parents to a 20-year-old daughter, Alabama Barker. The musician also has a stepdaughter—Atiana De La Hoya, 27, whom Moakler had with boxer Oscar De La Hoya.

When asked if she was planning to house the couple's entire family under one roof, Kardashian appeared to have mixed feelings.

"I love it in theory, but I think it's really hard with everybody of all different ages," she said. "It's a much different balance."

Although all of the children may not end up living in the home full time, Kardashian confirmed there will be plenty of space for everyone to make memories in one way or another.

"We're working toward something amazing," she said. "I can't wait to cook and have movie nights, barbecues. I want to make it feel magical for everybody. It's so fun."

Kardashian and Barker have previously opened up about the arrangements they've made to suit their large family. In a 2024 episode of "The Kardashians," which was filmed when she was pregnant with Rocky in 2023, she discussed their plans to move into Barker's nearby Calabasas estate.

"We're going to move into his house, which is a block away, and then redo my house at the same time so that we can all be together, living under one roof with the baby," she said.

Barker opened the doors of that very house to Architectural Digest in May 2022, revealing a surprisingly laid-back aesthetic for a rocker whose vibe typically leans much edgier.

"I've had homes with lots of flashy cars, murals, and bikes hanging from the ceiling. But with three children of my own, plus Kourtney's kids, this place felt right for this moment in my life," he said. "I wanted a house where I can rest and enjoy my family, a place where we can create memories."

AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Shares in artificial intelligence and technology firms have recovered slightly from an enormous sell-off that wiped trillions of dollars off stock values.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq stock index closed in positive territory following a market rout that began in the US on Thursday before spilling over to Asian and European markets into Friday.

Investors had been selling off tech shares in part due to concerns the Democrat victory in the midterm elections would mean slower artificial intelligence development.

However the latest US employment figures added to a mixed picture for American markets on Friday morning, with an unexpectedly strong 165,000 jobs added to the economy in September.

On Thursday, businesses closely linked to artificial intelligence development took a hammering on stock markets, particularly chip and semiconductor makers.

But the Nasdaq Composite bounced back slightly on Friday, ending the day up 1.1%, partly reversing some of its 5.5% fall.

The S&P 500, which tracks the share prices of the largest publicly-traded US companies, closed up 0.3%, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average was flat through the day before ending slightly down 0.4%.

Companies that lost hundreds of billions in market value recovered some ground on Friday, including Nvidia, SpaceX and Tesla.

However, stock markets in Asia and Europe fared worse.

The Kospi in South Korea closed down 6% on Friday, with shares in chipmaker SK Hynix plunging more than 10%. European markets also fell, with the main indexes in Germany, France and Spain ending the day down.

In the UK, the FTSE 100, which tracks the largest companies on the London Stock Exchange, declined 1.01%.

In addition to the recent market volatility, UK traders were also digesting the latest Bank of England decision to raise interest rates and the signal that another rate rise could be on the cards to curb rising prices.

The market fluctuations also come after the sweeping Democrat victory in the midterm elections.

A number of Democrats campaigned for new rules and regulations for artificial intelligence (AI) and data centres, and the party has since won control of both houses of congress as well as key contests in states that have been part of the AI data centre race.

US President Donald Trump on Friday reiterated his supported for what he calls "super intelligence", saying only "foolish leadership" would "say no to the riches they bring".

However, there have also been concerns over whether the enormous amounts of money being spent on AI will pay off.

Russ Shaw, founder of global tech advocacy firm Tech London Advocates, said the election result was sending a message that a rethink was needed on AI.

"The incoming Congress is going to really focus on AI in a very dramatic way," he told the BBC's Business Today.

"I'm a big fan of AI, but I'm watching what these companies are doing, and they're not communicating effectively at all, particularly in the US, about what the benefits of AI are all about."

Pierre BaquÃ©, chief executive of AI platform Neural Concept, said the share movements were a "necessary market correction" that reflected a legitimate concern about the amount of money being poured into AI infrastructure.

"The industry must move beyond unchecked infrastructure investment and demonstrate greater financial discipline," he said.

"We are entering a critical transition phase, a market purgatory where raw tech must prove its widespread economic value."

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said it had been a brutal week for markets, and the key question was whether the jump in the S&P 500 and Nasdaq would be sustained.

"Once investors have had the weekend to catch their breath, next week could give a clearer indication of whether the tech trade is going down the pan or fighting back."

Could the US midterms put a pin in the AI bubble?

Elon Musk appears to signal the end of the road for Cybertruck

As well as the fallout of the midterms and the plunge in artificial intelligence stocks, US traders will also be digesting the latest jobs figures.

And those numbers were better than expected: US employers added 165,000 new jobs in October, well above the average monthly increase and more than double what analysts had expected.

Overall the unemployment rate, which counts the number of people who are out of work but actively looking for a job, eased slightly to 4.1% from 4.2% in September.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) said the unemployment rate has remained in the narrow range of 4.1% to 4.3% since March.

Neil Birrell, chief investment officer at investment firm Premier Miton, said while the jobs figures may feel like second billing to the market movements they were still important.

"The US economy is running hard which may mean the risks the markets are worrying about at the moment are not such a risk to the real economy as is being feared," he said.

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

The U.S. Treasury Department has moved to sever crypto exchange MEXC Global from the American financial system, accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to Iran, North Korea and Russia.

In an order issued Friday through its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, Treasury said MEXC, one of the world’s 20 largest crypto exchanges, served as a major conduit for sanctions evasion and cybercrime.

The action, part of a broader effort dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing any funds transfers to or from the exchange. It takes effect 30 days after publication in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a statement, vowing to keep severing the regime’s “economic lifelines” and its attempts to use digital assets to dodge sanctions.

Founded in 2018, MEXC processes more than $1 billion in daily transactions, holds around $5 billion in reserves, and offers more than 3,000 tokens, according to FinCEN. Between May 2021 and May 2026, FinCEN said, the platform moved at least $16 billion connected to illicit activity, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion, with $232 million in direct transactions with sanctioned entities. The agency named MEXC a “primary money laundering concern” in connection with Russian illicit finance.

Despite publicly claiming anti-money-laundering controls and barring users in places like Iran and North Korea, MEXC’s compliance was riddled with “severe deficiencies,” FinCEN said.

The exchange was still transacting with sanctioned Russian exchange Garantex and its successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after Garantex was first designated. It also processed roughly $200 million linked to the A7A5 stablecoin and about $22 million benefiting Iran’s central bank and its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Those connections place MEXC squarely within a sprawling sanctions-evasion ecosystem that Treasury has been dismantling piece by piece.

The department last month designated Russia’s A7 Network a transnational criminal organization and proposed cutting its front companies off from U.S. finance, flagging more than $179 billion in transactions through its ruble-backed A7A5 token, much of it routed through Garantex and Grinex.

The same campaign has reached into Iran’s crypto sector, where Treasury gained sweeping authority to sanction anyone operating in it and blacklisted the Iranian exchange Nobitex over its role in funneling crypto to the IRGC.

The order uses a provision of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act rather than a standard sanctions designation, a tool FinCEN has increasingly reached for to cut off crypto infrastructure that operates outside the correspondent banking system.

'It came out of nowhere. I was shocked and sad': He was the face of Hunt: Showdown for years, then Crytek laid him off

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Bence "Beadyboy" Hamos reflects on 8 years at Crytek helping build Hunt: Showdown's community.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Hunt: Showdown community received a sucker punch in September when it learned that Bence "Beadyboy" Hamos, a beloved community-facing figure for the long-running extraction shooter, was unceremoniously let go in a wave of Crytek layoffs that affected at least 30 people.

For years Hamos was one of the faces of Hunt: Showdown, hosting livestreams, vlogs, and social media videos that kept players abreast of their favorite game's happenings. Through the highs and lows of Hunt's updates, events, and occasional controversies, he was a steady presence—calm, exuberant, and in touch with the experience of average players.

Hamos began at Crytek in 2018 in a social media role, eventually climbing to senior broadcast manager. As someone who absorbs the work of community managers constantly for work, I believe Hamos was particularly good at it—which is why his sudden departure hit Hunt's most dedicated players hard.

Hamos grew up in Budapest, Hungary. He'd looked up to Crytek since he was a teenager playing Crysis with his brother. He moved to Germany to find work and jumped at a job posting at the famed studio in 2018. He worked there for nearly nine years, focused entirely on Hunt: Showdown.

I caught up with Hamos last month, soon after his last day at Crytek, to learn what it was like to cultivate Hunt's community over nine years and talk about his unexpected departure.

"I really looked up to Crytek," Hamos told me. "[Getting hired] was such an unbelievable moment in my life, which obviously completely changed my whole career. It was nothing I never imagined I'd do. I was super happy."

Hamos said he had never worked in games when he interviewed at Crytek, but managed to charm the studio with his experience managing his rock band's social media, interacting with its community, and running livestreams on Ustream.

"It was right before the launch of Hunt, so in 2018," Hamos, who had recently moved to Germany from his home in Budapest to look for work, recounted. "I saw an opportunity at Crytek for a very entry-level social media position, and they really needed something.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

"Somehow, by huge luck, I got hired in two weeks."

When Hamos arrived at Crytek in early 2018, the industry was in the middle of a major shift toward live service games. Fortnite had yet to become the biggest game in the world, battle passes didn't exist outside mobile and MOBAs, and Crytek was taking a big chance on an FPS format so early in its infancy that it didn't have an established name yet: the extraction shooter. There was no playbook for communications in a game that's always changing, so Hamos did what came naturally.

To deal with a community and manage a community, you have to be part of a community

"I always had this principle that to deal with a community and manage a community, you have to be part of a community. So that's why I played a bunch of Hunt," he said. "I played over 2,000 or 3,000 hours just in my personal account, not even my testing account. I streamed it in my personal time.

"I think it's super important in a position like that to not just understand what the company wants, and the vision for the company, but also the other side of the equation—what players are feeling. I would understand their frustration. I would see the issues firsthand."

Hamos says his extensive hours in Hunt put him in a sometimes "weird" position of representing the interests of both the players and devs, which did not always align.

"You're the voice of the community, and you have to push that to the dev team and let them know about feedback and issues people are talking about. But at the same time, you also push the vision of the team and answers to the community from the dev side. It's a weird mixture."

I was curious what that process actually looked like, behind the scenes. How would a community manager package up player sentiment and deliver it to devs who aren't as deeply embedded in Reddit or YouTube?

"So we would collect reports on a weekly or bi-weekly basis, depending on the time period, and we would have to actually measure and collect it. Like, 'Hey, this many players are positive about this subject. These are the mixed ones. These are the negative ones.' And we list them out and say '200 people are saying this is bad.' That's a high priority from the community side, and you want to look at different platforms too, like socials, Steam, Reddit, Discord, and even your personal DMs, because not everyone has an X or BlueSky account. Those are all counted in, but it has to be accurate."

Hamos also explained how being the go-between for players and devs sometimes means taking a popular idea to the team and getting a no.

"There may be things that the community is really asking for, but the team is definitely saying no to. Whatever the reason: It may be not in line with the vision of the game or simply technical reasons, it may take too much time, and you really have to prioritize things because live service games always need updates, and people always want the big shiny things," he said. "When that happens, you just have to be transparent with the community and not ignore it."

The other side of that coin is calibrating feedback (especially balance feedback) from platforms that are prone to hyperbole.

"When you talk about a game like Hunt, you have to understand that the vocal part of the community may be veteran players that have been playing for a long time, and may be the highest-skill six star players, but that's actually a minority of all players. So you cannot necessarily balance the game around 1% or even less than 1% of the player base when your numbers show that it's not actually a problem for the community," he said.

"If you're talking about balancing a weapon [for example], designers look at the percentage of people using it in every skill level. Sometimes it may look very loud on Reddit that, say, the weapon is way too overpowered, but when you look at the data, it may be actually a super low percentage of people using it. They would then decide to make a small change or even show the data to players.

You cannot necessarily balance the game around 1% or even less than 1% of the player base...

"It has happened before where we said, 'Hey guys, here's the usage rate, this is why we are not planning on changing it, and it's not actually as big of a deal as you may perceive it," he said. "I think the community appreciates it when they can see the numbers behind things."

But Hamos also offered an example of an adjustment made with high-skill players in mind that didn't really affect the rest of the community: Making Spitzer ammo, a special bullet type that increases bullet velocity, a scarce lootable item instead of something you can buy from the store whenever.

"Today, Hunt has so many layers and so much depth that you can really make small adjustments that may not affect lower-skill players," he said. " I always had a lot of trust and respect for the designers. They really know what they are doing, so I think Hunt and the community are in good hands in this regard."

Hamos had nothing but amazing things to say about his coworkers and experience working at Crytek, even if the way he left was, in his words, shocking and sad.

"It came out of nowhere. And after nine years, I did feel like a part of my soul was ripped out because it was my day-to-day. I kind of lived and breathed the game. I would wake up and think about Hunt and work on Hunt. It has been a part of my entire adult life, basically," he said. "So I was shocked and sad, and I'm still sad. I still get a bit emotional about it because it's painful, but I don't have any hard feelings."

His firing was part of a wider round of layoffs at Crytek, the second the studio has been through since February 2025. Hamos understands that the game industry is a "pretty rough space" right now, with tens of thousands of developers displaced in the last three years, and showed no sign of bitterness—in keeping with this jolly demeanor in countless Hunt update videos. On his last official day, Hamos posted a farewell message on the official Hunt subreddit—it quickly became the top post on the page with thousands of upvotes and hundreds of supportive comments. The response was "really special," as he recalled.

"To get faced with the fact that your work meant so much to people is something really special. I'm trying not to get emotional here, but when you work day-to-day, you don't really feel that. But now when it's over, it is kind of a payoff for all of that, which really hits like a truck," he said. "It's not something that I expected or maybe even deserved, but something that meant a lot to me."

Unlike countless colleagues now left in the lurch by layoffs, Hamos was fortunate enough to quickly land a new gig, thanks in large part to his recognizable community work on Hunt. In October, he became head of community and social at Good Fun Corporation, a studio formed by former Hell Let Loose developers. They're making Hunger: an old-timey extraction game with distinct similarities to Hunt.

It came out of nowhere. And after nine years, I did feel like a part of my soul was ripped out because it was my day-to-day. I kind of lived and breathed the game.

"Of course there are similar vibes to Hunt in Hunger, though it's an extraction RPG, which is very different. It has a lot of MMO elements to it and progression and questing, which appeals to me a lot."

Hamos is starting over from scratch, and his highest hope is to cultivate a community as friendly, passionate, and sturdy as Hunt: Showdown.

"I think [Hunt] is one of the least toxic communities. Of course there's some toxicity, but the people are more mature and understanding compared to most. And since it's a bit smaller overall, you also have this little family kind of vibe where we're talking to people who also understand the game and live the same hunter life."

Hamos told me the end of his time at Crytek doesn't mean the end of his own Hunt career. He still loves the game and believes its best days are ahead.

"Many games enter with a bang and then fade out quickly with fewer players unless they have a major update. Hunt, especially among live service shooters, is really an exception to the rule. You see growth forever—like it's still going," he said.

"I just wish the best for Hunt and hope it will stay for another 10 years at least."

US Treasury cuts off MEXC over alleged $16B in illicit transactions

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The US Treasury bureau FinCEN on Friday ordered US financial institutions to stop processing transfers involving MEXC Global.

The US Department of Treasury on Friday issued an order to cut cryptocurrency exchange MEXC Global off from the US’ financial system after accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in transactions linked to sanctions evasion, cybercrime and other illicit activity.

The Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) asserted that MEXC handled transactions involving sanctioned groups in Iran, Russia and North Korea between May 2021 and May 2026. This included around $1.5 billion in transactions linked to Russian sanctions evasion, according to the official press release.

The order effectively bans US financial institutions from processing money transfers involving MEXC and will take effect 30 days after it is published in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets.”

The action is part of Operation Economic Outcast, a US Treasury campaign launched in August with aims to cut off financial entites that help Iran evade sanctions.

According to DefiLlama, the exchange held about $4.48 billion in total assets as of Friday. Cointelegraph contacted MEXC for comment but had not received a response by publication.

Will AI slop overwhelm creators? Mark Rober isn’t worried–but his team is using AI as a “thought partner” in production

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mark Rober told Business Insider he thinks his content has a “moat” that will keep it safe from being affected by the AI slop deluge.

But, at the same time, he’s said his team is using generative AI as a “thought partner” for writing, and that it can make “the perfect meal” for students using his Class CrunchLabs modules.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

With BI, Rober addressed creators’ anxiety about AI-generated videos encroaching on the watch time that should go to their genuine content.

“Do you want to see a simulation of someone dunking a basketball with a sick trick, or do you want to see someone actually do it?” he told BI. “The fact that [viewers] know it’s real is what makes it crazy.”

He said creators should be reassured that “[o]ur brains developed for hundreds of thousands of years with human connection,” and viewers want “real and authentic” content filmed by people who interact with the world around them.

He also added that people need to retain their cognitive skills instead of just ChatGPTing everything. Both creators and viewers should investigate things themselves, using their own human brains.

“Curiosity is so important in this new age of AI,” he said. “It means you just have a lifetime of learning, versus like, ‘Oh, this thing knows the answer, so who cares?'”

But, again, Rober has implemented AI across his processes. This past June, he told The Information that AI “has the power to really adapt” educational settings.

“Basically what we’re doing with Class CrunchLabs is we’re making great ingredients, really fresh ingredients, and then AI can be like, ‘Hey, I have three kids in my class who are challenged this way, and they’re not great at reading, but these ones are. We’ve already covered this stuff.’ And it could take those ingredients and make the perfect meal for your class,” he explained. “I think it’s gonna be very good at that.”

He said that on the creator side, “I think it would allow me to scale myself in certain ways. We’re certainly using it as a thought partner on writing, on some thumbnail stuff.”

Like he did with BI, Rober said that in science content specifically, viewers probably want something real instead of something LLM’d. “I think it remains to be seen what the public’s appetite is for just pure AI-generated content,” he said. “I would think in science, you actually want to see the lava melt through the thing, versus it just being completely fake. But we’ll see.”

We’ve written before about how big tech platforms like YouTube have found themselves in a tricky catch-22: They want to make money off the AI bubble (uh oh), but as they roll out more generative tools and push creators to use them, they end up combating endless amounts of slop pumped out by those very same tools.

With Rober and other creators, we see something similar: They feel their content is safe from AI content made by other people, but also use AI in their processes, in the name of “scaling” or “ideating” or streamlining and cost-lessening production.

Rober’s team is using AI in its writing process, and a script is the foundation of long-form, educational YouTube content like his. Is the vibe just that viewers won’t care, so long as no fully AI-generated video makes it into an upload? Intriguing…
