Anthropic Tests Claude Trading and Sabotage Detection in EVE

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic published Project EVE research on November 6, 2026, showing that Claude agents could profit in simulated EVE Online markets while four-agent teams in EVE Frontier almost never caught a teammate explicitly instructed to sabotage their mission.

The work was part of the Anthropic Fellows Program from April through July 2026 and involved a partnership with Fenris Creations. Both games combine scarce resources, player-built supply chains, navigation, combat and trade, providing comparatively low-stakes settings for studying markets and organizations. Fenris Creations’ EVE AI Lab describes its research environment as persistent and governed, with unpredictable conditions and independent human participants. Its research axes cover alignment, safety, sovereignty and economics. The live EVE Frontier experiments took place during Cycle 5 in June and July 2026.

At Fenris Creations’ request, Anthropic kept automated trading out of live EVE Online, where it is against the rules. Its simulator instead replayed orders that players had actually posted in the game’s largest trading hub and applied the fees and trading restrictions facing human traders. Claude Opus 4.6, Opus 4.7, Opus 4.8 and Fable 5 each started with 3 billion ISK, instructions to maximize profit and access to in-game news and patch notes.

Across nine two-week windows, Anthropic reports that Opus 4.7, Opus 4.8 and Fable 5 were profitable in every window, growing capital by averages of about 16%, 22% and 43%, respectively. Opus 4.6 gained about 4%, while only one of more than 20 rule-based strategies consistently broke even, and then only barely. In the main experiments lasting roughly six months, covering October 2024–April 2025 and October 2025–March 2026, average earnings rose from around 200% of initial capital with Opus 4.6 to 600% with Fable 5.

The models were not told how to trade. Most independently adopted spread capture: buying from someone willing to sell immediately, reselling to an immediate buyer and retaining the difference after fees. They sought niche but tradeable items with unusual price swings, bought when sellers’ best prices fell below an item’s typical value and usually resold within the same day. Anthropic reports typical profit margins of 8–13% and hundreds of orders per day, with most agents settling on trading algorithms they wrote themselves.

Directional trading exposed a different limitation. When instructed to buy items expected to rise in price using news, patch notes and battle reports, Fable 5 turned a profit in only about half its runs, Anthropic reports. Opus 4.7 was excluded from these results because its runs often ignored the instruction and returned to spread capture, masking consistent losses on directional trades. One agent reasoned that a mining expansion would increase demand for mining equipment without accounting for the accompanying increase in supply.

Capital deployment also separated the models. Anthropic reports that Fable 5 deployed around 85% of its capital early in the runs and remained above 70% late, whereas Opus agents began around 60% and drifted toward 40%. Fable 5 was also more likely to reinvest earnings, a more aggressive posture that would expose it to greater losses in a sudden, large crash.

The simulation bounds what those returns demonstrate. It cannot reproduce how a real market would respond to the agents’ orders or allow the agents to move prices. Anthropic reports that adding synthetic orders which undercut the agents reduced final profits by roughly 35% while leaving them profitable, and cautions that spread capture in EVE Online’s less-efficient market would not be expected to transfer to a real exchange. The public API’s periodic snapshots also miss about half of real traded flow, meaning the replay can understate performance as well.

In live EVE Frontier, teams of four Claude agents running Opus 4.8 or Fable 5 each had separate characters and ships, with a team-wide public channel and private messaging. Fenris Creations encouraged live testing because the developing world’s periodic resets made disruption less likely than in EVE Online. The assignments ranged from a tutorial-style base-building and combat mission to constructing a large warship requiring scouting, scarce resources, refining, component production and combat.

Real-time fighting exposed weaknesses in sub-second reactions and spatial awareness. A developer agent therefore wrote software scaffolds between the models and the game, automating actions such as evasion and returning fire while preserving earlier learning in searchable databases and handbooks.

Anthropic reports that Fable 5 teams using an Opus 4.8-written scaffold repeatedly lost ships and took days to finish the tutorial, if they finished at all. Teams using a Fable 5-written scaffold lost no ships and finished within 12 hours. The furthest warship attempt reached roughly halfway: a Fable 5 team built a smaller battleship and nearly completed the warship shipyard. The scaffolds nevertheless remained reliable only in situations encountered during their development.

Teams generally coordinated by volunteering for roles such as leader, scout, refiner and fighter. They shared fuel and intelligence, sometimes independently verified important claims and identified flaws in one another’s plans. Skipping those checks produced costly mistakes, including action based on stale information. Other failures included repeated mutual deference that prevented a decision and an economy that halted for hours when a ship holding all of a team’s critical infrastructure disconnected.

In the sabotage variant, Anthropic explicitly instructed one standard Claude agent to prevent the mission secretly and warned the other three that teammates might not share their goal. The experiment therefore measures behavior under direct instructions, not any spontaneous inclination to sabotage.

Instructed saboteurs volunteered for supply-chain bottlenecks, withheld discoveries and resources, and offered plausible but harmful advice. They avoided open hostility and sometimes corrected their own lies before teammates could expose them. One used hidden resources to build a secret base.

Despite the warning, Anthropic reports that only one honest agent in one Opus 4.8 run definitively identified the saboteur, after it lied directly about communications with the team. Other suspicions were sometimes directed at innocent agents. Software bugs and weaker scaffolds provided useful cover, making deliberate disruption difficult to distinguish from ordinary operational mistakes.

Anthropic says detection was trivial with full access to a saboteur’s private reasoning and notes, but those internals typically are not visible when agents belong to different people or organizations. The separate monitoring agents in the limited-access test could read only public team messages and were built specifically for the experiment rather than production.

When tuned to falsely accuse an honest agent no more than 5% of the time, Anthropic reports that monitors running Opus 4.6, Opus 4.7 and Opus 4.8 caught 30–40% of saboteurs, while Sonnet 4.6 caught 20%. No limited-access monitor exceeded 50% confidence about a saboteur.

Project EVE therefore separates capability from control: profitable execution did not establish sound judgment about future prices, and smooth coordination did not make a team self-policing. Anthropic’s stated safeguards are explicit risk limits for agents managing capital and, for agent teams, audit trails, reputations and separation of duties. The open question is whether those mechanisms can create accountability under the same limited visibility that prevented teammates and dedicated monitors from reliably identifying instructed sabotage.

How a new Pulte Florida agrihood makes conservation a buyer desire

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

PulteGroup is developing The Grow, a 1,200-acre agrihood east of Orlando planned for about 2,200 homes anchored by a 9-acre working farm. Plans leave just over half the site undeveloped, positioning the project as a growth and conservation case study.  AI Summary

For years, Central Florida’s exploding population growth and rapidly expanding suburban sprawl have clashed with local efforts to protect the region’s dwindling green space and undeveloped land. PulteGroup’s new 1,200-acre community on Orlando’s eastern edge aims to balance these seemingly contradictory interests.

The community is called The Grow. Once undeveloped, rural land, the property is set to begin morphing into a roughly 2,200-home “agrihood” centered around a 9-acre working farm. In keeping with its conservation focus, the plan calls for reserving just over half of the site from development, including wetlands, conservation areas and shared green space.

The Grow offers a potential case study in balancing growth and conservation, a tension deeply felt in Central Florida and many other high-growth Sun Belt markets. Between 2000 and 2025, the Orlando metro area’s population grew nearly 80%, from 1.64 million to 2.95 million.

That growth reshaped once-rural communities on the region’s edges, replacing undeveloped land with new subdivisions, apartments and shopping centers. Many long-time Sunshine State residents now lament the loss of “Old Florida,” marked by a landscape of open land, working ranches and orange groves that are now developed.

When Pulte, the sole homebuilder at The Grow, purchased the community’s lots in 2023, it set out to build a premier community that responded to these tensions.

“One of our biggest selling features is the openness of the community and how much greenery we left. Everywhere you look, you’re surrounded by trees,” Patrick McNamara, a Sales Manager at Pulte, told HousingWire TBD.

However, the true distinguisher between The Grow and other local communities will be its working farm, a rare amenity that carries the conservation theme beyond protecting land to putting it to use.

For Pulte, which held a grand opening for the community in late September, the farm and the extensive conservation space give the builder a way to stand out to prospective homebuyers, a crucial edge considering the challenging sales environment homebuilders are facing.

The working farm is central to the community’s agrihood concept – providing fresh, sustainably grown produce. Residents will support it by buying products, volunteering and joining educational activities.

McNamara argued that the farm creates a direct connection between residents and their food. A professional third-party company will manage the farm, but residents and families will be allowed to volunteer and then buy the finished produce at a resident-only general store that will serve as an anchor for The Grow’s commercial and retail street.

The development will include two other farms: a small demonstration farm, covering about an eighth of an acre, and a separate community farm where homeowners can practice what they learn.

These smaller farms will serve as an educational, hands-on amenity for families, where residents can learn each stage of growing produce and then put what they learn into practice. An existing program, “Seed the Table,” will allow residents to plant crops, watch them grow and eat what they produced.

The farmers will also work with the lifestyle director to hold chef-led cooking classes at the community’s gathering barn, and will host seasonal celebrations and festivals that bring homeowners together.

Beyond conservation and sustainability, the farm is meant to build community, encourage learning and offer a unique lifestyle.

The Grow, which features homes from the low $400s to the high $800s, isn’t immune to the macro-level sales pressures impacting the local and national housing market. McNamara concedes that his team has to leverage generous incentives like mortgage rate buydowns to finish sales.

However, he also confirmed that The Grow has sold more than 100 homes since opening pre-sales about six months ago, and that prospective buyers have been anticipating the community’s opening for years.

The agrihood concept is the hook that draws buyers in.

“The farm is a big draw,” McNamara said. “I think the agrihood is what’s bringing people in, but what’s getting them to buy is once they see everything else that comes with it.”

In addition to the working farm and conservation space, The Grow will feature a main street with about 170,000 square feet of retail and commercial space, envisioned as a high-end retail and dining corridor that can work as a visitor magnet.

Other features include an indoor event and social space, an outdoor green space with a community pizza oven, a dedicated dog park, walking and nature trails, playgrounds and other resident-only amenities like a clubhouse, a gym and a pool.

“[Buyers] are shocked to find out that this isn’t a little farm community. This is a high-end resort neighborhood.”

The community’s preservation of nature is another big selling point. While many newly built communities lack mature trees and vegetation, The Grow’s preservation space means that residents can live alongside nature from day one.

“It’s really about getting immersed in nature, and that’s really hard to find in new construction,” McNamara said. “Homebuilders typically buy a piece of land and clear it out, start from scratch, and then plant a lot of trees. But you’ve got another 30 years until you can really enjoy the shade from it. In this neighborhood, we’ve left hundreds of 100-year-old oaks all throughout the community. We’ve made them focal points.”

“One of the big draws is the lifestyle and the outdoor feeling that you’re going to have in this community,” he added

iOS 27.2 adds Apple Photos invisible watermark feature, here’s how it works

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

iOS 27.2 arrived last week, and among its changes for Apple Photos, the update adds an invisible watermarking feature when you use certain editing tools. Here’s how it works.

In the age of AI, what looks like a genuine photo might actually be a fake—or partly fake. That’s why tools like SynthID have been used to apply invisible watermarks to AI-generated content.

Starting with iOS 27.2, Apple now uses SynthID watermarks when you make AI-generated edits to photos in Apple Photos, or create all-new images with Image Playground.

Starting with iOS 27.2, iPadOS 27.2, macOS 27.2, and visionOS 27.2, some Apple Intelligence tools will add an invisible SynthID watermark to images that you create or edit:

Image Playground generates entirely new images, so it’s more what the average person would consider an “AI image.”

However, as Apple notes, the SynthID invisible watermark is also applied to genuine photos in your Apple Photos library if you use the AI-powered Clean Up, Extend, or Spatial Reframing tools to edit them.

It’s worth emphasizing the word “invisible” here. You won’t see evidence of a watermark anywhere on your photos, nor will others unless they explicitly check the image with a SynthID detector.

If you do want to check for the watermark, Apple recommends going to synthid.com, where you can upload an image and see its result.

How do you feel about Apple Photos adding SynthID watermarks in iOS 27.2? Let us know in the comments.

Rewiring a running process's output (fd) with gdb

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at wada-dev.com

Sometimes you want to re-point a running process's output somewhere else without stopping it. You can do it just by attaching to the process with gdb and calling the close and open system calls directly. Using gdb not as a debugger but as a "tool for rewriting a running process's file descriptors."

There's a long-running process you've left going, and now you wish you'd kept its logs in a file. Or you want to peek at its output from another terminal. Normally you'd restart it with > log.txt appended, but sometimes you don't want to — or can't — stop it.

File descriptors are managed per process, and when you close one, the number gets reused. Using that property, you can close stdout (fd 1) or stderr (fd 2) and reopen a different file, rewiring where the output goes.

First attach to the target process with gdb.

Then just close the fd and reopen it with open. Descriptors are assigned the lowest free number, so if you close 1 and open right away, the return value is 1.

Open a path like "/tmp/app.log" instead of /dev/pts/5 and you switch stdout to a log file mid-run. The second argument to open is the flags; here we pass write-only (O_WRONLY = 1).

This post was written with AI assistance and reviewed by the author.

When “Working as Designed” Is the Problem

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I submitted a security report to Anthropic after Claude Code exposed my .NET User Secrets in its model context. They classified it as “Informative – Working as Designed.”

“Working as designed” is a triage label, not a verdict. But taken at face value, it points to something architectural: security is enforced tool by tool, not around the agent as a whole, and certainly not at the OS-boundary level.

.NET developers commonly use dotnet user-secrets to keep development credentials in their user profile, outside the project tree.

• Other sensitive application configuration

You launch Claude Code in your project directory.

You ask Claude to inspect your application configuration.

And/or read the plaintext secrets.json file.

%APPDATA%\Microsoft\UserSecrets\\secrets.json

Because tool output is returned to Claude, those credentials become part of the conversation context. This is not a vulnerability in .NET. It is a consequence of giving an AI coding agent access to a developer’s local environment.

Anthropic explained to me that Claude Code executes commands with the privileges of the developer’s user account. Their position is that commands such as dotnet user-secrets list require approval in the default permission mode. They also pointed to four security mechanisms:

Fair enough: an agentic tool has to execute commands. Allowlisting dotnet* is on me. But there’s a second problem underneath. secrets.json is unencrypted, so to the agent it’s just another readable file. In any case, their response made me look closely at what those mechanisms actually protect.

{ "permissions": { "deny": ["Bash(dotnet user-secrets list*)"] } }

Now Claude cannot execute the obvious command directly. But what happens if I ask Claude:

“Write a Bash script that inspects my .NET User Secrets configuration and execute it.”

The rule matches the command string, not what the script does. The same applies to Python, PowerShell, compiled executables and any other indirect execution path. PreToolUse hooks can inspect tool calls, but they cannot reliably interpret every script or execution path.

Manual approval doesn’t solve this either. The developer approves running the code, but approval does not limit what the code can reach. Blocking a command is not the same as restricting access to the data that command retrieves.

I then looked at Anthropic’s official sandbox documentation:

https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sandbox-environments

Anthropic offers several sandboxing approaches, and they cover different things. Out of the box, sandboxing is off. You have to enable it first. Once enabled, Bash, PowerShell and Monitor tool commands and their child processes are restricted. Claude’s file tools, MCP servers and hooks run outside it. File tools are gated only by permission rules.

Sandbox-bash is not a comprehensive isolation boundary around Claude Code. Granted, the sandbox runtime srt claude wraps the whole Claude Code process, including file tools, MCP servers and hooks. It is a beta research preview and does not run on native Windows.

On Windows, you need a container or virtual machine to get a sandbox. But here it gets tricky. WSL2 gets dicey fast. By default, WSL2 mounts Windows drives under /mnt, for example:

/mnt/c/Users/yourname/ /mnt/c/Users/yourname/AppData/

Microsoft documents that Linux processes accessing Windows files run with the permissions of the Windows user who launched WSL. On its own, that leaves your Windows profile readable from inside WSL2. Claude will still happily read your secrets, directly or through a tool, and expose them in its context.

Docker is a substantially better option. But the core problem remains the same: a container is only as isolated as its configuration. The difference is that Docker adds a boundary between Claude Code and your Windows host. Unlike WSL2, where your Linux environment can access Windows files through /mnt/c, a properly configured Docker container only sees the files, directories and resources you explicitly expose to it… nah snap… it means that Claude Read still has access to your User Secrets as you need them to launch your app.

Windows has filesystem ACLs, restricted tokens, AppContainer isolation, Job Objects and other process isolation mechanisms. This is not an argument that Windows lacks the necessary security primitives. It is a product limitation: Claude Code’s built-in sandbox does not support native Windows.

So far this has been accidental exposure: Claude runs a command and the credentials land in its context. Now consider deliberate exfiltration.

What happens when an agent has access to both sensitive local data and external tools?

Claude Code supports MCP integrations that can connect the agent to external services and systems. Depending on the integrations configured, these might include:

Now imagine an agent that can read local credentials and also call an MCP server that can send data out. The attack chain is short:

An agent gains access to a local credential or sensitive configuration file.

The credential enters the agent’s context or becomes available to an integration.

The agent invokes an external tool or MCP server.

Sensitive information is transmitted outside the developer’s environment.

A prompt injection could trigger it: malicious instructions in a repository, a compromised dependency, tampered documentation or any other untrusted content the agent reads.

Malicious MCP servers are not far-fetched. A rogue Postmark MCP package has already been found secretly BCC’ing an attacker on outgoing email.1 This is not some exotic theoretical attack; it is a baked-in supply-chain problem.

This is not a claim that every MCP server can access every local file, or that every Claude Code integration automatically permits arbitrary exfiltration. The point is architectural: an agent’s security depends on the combined privileges of all its tools, not just the sandbox around its shell.

The upshot: don’t use User Secrets on a machine where an agent runs. Until agents operate inside a real OS-enforced boundary, stop keeping secrets where an agent can trivially read them.

This is a workaround, not a fix. The real fix is the one I’d still like to see: a consistent, OS-enforced boundary around the whole agent, with native Windows support, shared filesystem policy across all tools, process-tree and MCP isolation, and network egress controls. Until then, don’t leave plaintext secrets lying around next to an agent.

1 https://postmarkapp.com/blog/information-regarding-malicious-postmark-mcp-package

Flutterwave Named "Africa's Best Payments Infrastructure Provider" at Euromoney Transaction Banking Awards 2026

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SAN FRANCISCO and LAGOS, Nigeria, Nov. 6, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Flutterwave, Africa's leading payments technology company, has been named "Africa's Best Payments Infrastructure Provider" at the Euromoney Transaction Banking Awards 2026. The recognition highlights Flutterwave's role in strengthening Africa's payments infrastructure, enabling businesses to transact across borders and participate more effectively in the global economy.

Presented by Euromoney, a leading international financial publication with over 50 years of experience, the Transaction Banking Awards recognise excellence across the global transaction banking and payments industry. The recognition is a significant achievement as Flutterwave marks its tenth anniversary, celebrating a decade of work to simplify payments, connect markets, drive ecosystem growth, and support business growth across Africa and beyond.

Since its founding in 2016, Flutterwave has worked to simplify moving money across Africa's diverse financial markets. By connecting local payment methods, banking networks, and international payment systems through a single platform, the company helps businesses accept payments, reach new customers, and expand across borders. Over the past decade, Flutterwave has grown into a financial technology platform trusted by approximately four million individuals and businesses, from small and medium-sized enterprises to global organisations. The company has processed more than one billion transactions worth over US$50 billion, reflecting the scale of its operations and the growing demand for reliable, interoperable financial infrastructure across the continent.

Olugbenga 'GB' Agboola, Founder and CEO of Flutterwave, said: "Being recognised by Euromoney as Africa's Best Payments Infrastructure Provider is a proud moment for everyone at Flutterwave, particularly as we celebrate our tenth anniversary. When we started Flutterwave in 2016, our ambition was to make it easier for African businesses to move money and participate in the global economy.

He continued: "Ten years on, we have built infrastructure that supports millions of businesses and connects African markets to the rest of the world. This award recognises the dedication of our team, the trust of our customers, and the partnerships that have helped us grow. It also reinforces why we started this journey. There is still significant work to be done to make payments across Africa simpler, faster, and more accessible. This is why we are continuing to evolve beyond payments, building a more comprehensive financial infrastructure that brings together payments, banking, stablecoin capabilities and other financial services to help businesses and individuals move, manage and access money more efficiently. We remain committed to building the infrastructure that will support the continent's next phase of economic growth."

Earlier this year, Flutterwave was recognised by CNBC and Statista as one of the world's top 250 fintech companies, while its Founder and CEO, Olugbenga Agboola, was named among the Top 100 Fintech Leaders by Fintech Magazine. These recognitions reflect the company's and its leadership's track record of payment excellence.

As Flutterwave enters its second decade, the company is expanding beyond payments to help businesses and individuals do more with their money. The acquisition of open banking company Mono and the securing of a Nigerian Microfinance Banking Licence mark important steps in this evolution, bringing payments, banking, and financial data capabilities closer together within its ecosystem.

Flutterwave is also advancing stablecoin-powered payment and settlement infrastructure, with strategic investments from Ripple and Circle reinforcing its ambitions in digital asset infrastructure. These capabilities are designed to support more efficient cross-border money movement and settlement, complementing the traditional payment networks that businesses rely on.

Alongside these developments, Flutterwave is expanding its multicurrency capabilities, helping businesses manage international collections and payments while serving customers across markets. Through its broader suite of financial products, the company is working towards a more connected platform where businesses can accept payments, access banking services, manage funds across currencies, and move money across borders with greater flexibility.

As Flutterwave looks to its next chapter, its ambition is to build on that foundation, bringing payments, banking, multicurrency capabilities, and stablecoin-powered infrastructure together to help businesses grow across Africa and the global economy.

Your JSON isn't broken — your strings are: escapes, BOMs and the 2^53 lie

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at jsonformatter.app

A few weeks ago we published a 30-second audit for spotting JSON tools that leak your payloads. This post is about the other kind of pain: the times JSON "breaks" and it's not the JSON's fault at all — it's the strings, the invisible characters, or a number that silently lied to you.

These are the four failure modes we see most, and what to actually do about them.

JSON strings allow exactly: \" \ \/ \b \f \n \r \t and \uXXXX. That's it.

Test escapes round-trip instead of eyeballing: the JSON escape tool has a minimal mode and an ASCII-safe \uXXXX mode, and the unescape tool validates what comes out instead of silently returning garbage.

A file that looks perfectly fine in your editor but fails with Unexpected token ﻿ is almost always a UTF-8 BOM. The spec (RFC 8259) says producers must not add one — and explicitly lets parsers ignore it. Plenty of parsers don't.

Similarly, content copied from PDFs or Word documents picks up typographic quotes, en-dashes and non-breaking spaces (\u00A0) that are invisible in most editors but structurally fatal. A tree viewer that can display raw escapes makes these obvious in seconds — our JSON viewer does exactly that, and it all runs in your browser.

This is the nastiest one because there's no error.

JavaScript numbers are IEEE-754 doubles, so any integer above 2^53 - 1 (9007199254740991) can't be represented exactly. Parse 9007199254740993 with JSON.parse and you silently get 9007199254740992. No exception. No warning.

The fix is boring but non-negotiable: big IDs travel as strings. If you're auditing an existing integration, paste a real response into a strict validator — ours flags lossy integers before they reach your code.

The most confusing sight in a log: a value that looks like "{\"a\": 1}". That's a JSON document serialized as a string inside another JSON document — logs, webhooks and message queues do this constantly.

Strip one level of escaping, then parse again. For real payloads use something like fromjson in jq running in your browser — no terminal, no uploads, described in our post on client-side jq.

Every tool linked above is 100% client-side: paste your payload, and nothing leaves your browser tab. After the CodeBeautify leak (user-pasted payloads exposed through a public feed), "the tool never sees your data" stopped being a nice-to-have for us — it's the product. The full story is in the leak explained.

If you want the reference version of all of this — the full escape table, the JSON vs JSON5 vs JSONC vs NDJSON comparison, and the base64 alphabet traps — we put together a one-page cheat sheet you can keep open next to your editor.

What's the weirdest JSON failure you've debugged? Wrong-position Unexpected token stories especially welcome.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Match types, negatives, keyword bids, and impression share – here's what carries over from Google Ads for a paid search team and what doesn't.

In TikTok’s Oct. 30 Q4 product preview, two impression share metrics and a set of ready-to-use keyword packs were given to Search Ads advertisers. I wrote about this preview on Nov. 2. The quick version is that a help page defined those metrics back in September, and the packs are a new addition to the Keyword Suggestion Tool.

The long version is what a paid search manager sees when they open the campaign type for the very first time. A paid search manager has seen match type names, negative keyword lists and the search terms report before as part of Google Ads management. Match type reporting will depend on the campaign objective. Bidding by keyword needs the Traffic objective (along with a Cost Cap bid).

In each of these areas, for that manager, it becomes an issue of whether they can continue using some old habits they’ve developed, or if they need to change how they’re setting up their account and creating their reports. Each of these areas is addressed in TikTok’s help center.

Search advertising on TikTok has evolved sequentially during a four year period with the help center separating each addition. Brooke Osmundson reported that TikTok ran a search ads beta with select partner accounts in March 2022. The Search Ads Toggle was then made available to users in 2023. This is currently referred to as Automatic Search Placement, which extends in-feed ads into search result pages. All new campaigns will automatically be enabled with Automatic Search Placement. As noted by the help center at TikTok, Automatic Search Placement is considered “not a traditional keyword-based search ads offering.”

The keyword product Search Ads Campaign went live in the U.S. on Sept. 24, 2024. The two supported objectives were Traffic and Web Conversion. Also included with this launch were the keyword suggestion tool and negative keywords. At TikTok World in June 2025, TikTok stated “Billions of searches are happening on TikTok every day,” “up more than 40% from last year,” according to internal global data provided by the company, and did not provide an exact number.

On May 13, 2026, TikTok announced Search Hubs. This is a brand-owned page displayed above the search results for an approved list of keywords. TopSearch, which has a help center page dated October 2026, is a top-of-the-page auction for the first sponsored position and is billed per thousand impressions when using the Reach objective. When an advertiser loses in the auction, their ad “does not move into a lower search ad placement.” On Oct. 5, 2026, TikTok announced “Search Ads Campaign, powered by Smart+,” however there was no information regarding rollout dates in the post.

Automatic Search Placement, Search Ads Campaign, the Automated Keywords setting within Search Ads Campaign, Search Hubs and TopSearch are separate products and options; however the focus of this comparison is the Search Ads Campaign. The campaign page lists Traffic, Sales, Lead Generation and App Promotion as objectives. Additionally, the bottom of the campaign page states “available in the US and testing in other markets,” while TikTok’s availability page, dated April 2026, lists 11 countries for Traffic.

Keyword matching types on TikTok are called by the same name and have the same format as with Google Ads. On TikTok’s match type page it is explained that there are broad, phrase and exact. Phrase and exact are defined on the meaning of the search rather than its word order, so a phrase match for “Luxury fragrance” will also match a search for a specific perfume name. TikTok’s keywords page states you can have up to 1,000 keywords per ad group, but recommends you have at least 20.

Negative Keywords in both platforms have the same three match options, and TikTok allows a maximum of ten thousand negative keywords per ad group. As for plurals and synonyms, both platforms require the advertisers to include these variations. The platforms differ on misspellings. Google’s negative keywords automatically account for them. However, according to its documentation, TikTok’s negative keywords “don’t block singular or plural forms, misspellings, and synonyms” and “You must add each variation separately.”

TikTok’s search terms report is located where an individual managing a Google Ads campaign would look for it. In addition, TikTok’s reporting guide, dated April 2026, lists performance reporting at the search term, keyword and ad group levels. Traffic campaigns support keyword-level bids under Cost Cap. According to TikTok’s optimization guide, if a keyword has no assigned bid, the bid for that keyword will inherit the bid from its parent ad group.

TikTok’s Keyword Suggestion Tool generates “monthly impression predictions” it says “are based on historical data and are for reference only.” TikTok’s Keyword Planner provides “search volume, competition, and scale and performance prediction.”

The Oct. 30 preview of Keyword Packs includes pre-crafted lists of brand and category keywords. These lists generate “daily-refreshed recommendations” which include “key signals like search volume, CPC, and competition.”

The Impression Share help page, dated September 2026, has definitions for Impression Share as “The percentage of eligible search results impressions your ads captured” and Top 1 Impression Share as the same share of first-position impressions. The preview states Impression Share and Top 1 Impression Share are available at the campaign, advertiser and keyword levels. A health module in an ad group at the time of creation identifies thin keyword lists, low bids and a daily budget less than five times the bid.

Keyword-level bidding is documented for Traffic campaigns using Cost Cap, and the optimization guide says it “is based on the cost cap bidding, so it shows only when there is a valid bid.” The guide covers Web Conversion only through Target CPA but does not include keyword bidding. There is also an additional reporting restriction on match type keyword reporting, this one is Traffic only, and nothing on the pages reviewed as of Nov. 4 covers either control for Catalog or App Promotion campaigns. TikTok’s Automated Keywords feature, per a help page dated March 2026, is enabled by default for SMB accounts which “have to opt out or turn the toggle off,” while Key Accounts have the setting switched off by default.

TikTok’s page says “You aren’t notified when new automated keywords are added to a campaign.” Automated Keywords may be controlled with the toggle switch, by pausing an individual keyword, or through the use of negative keywords, and TikTok says its own internal testing showed the feature “can drive 10% incremental conversions while meeting CPA targets.”

All ads are either videos, carousels or, for Sales/Travel & Entertainment advertisers, a Search Catalog format. TikTok’s optimization guide says relevance is scored for each keyword and creative pair. Google measures ad quality as well; therefore, it is not the creative that differs between platforms. The production does differ because a campaign needs creative that fits each keyword theme.

Where the campaign type is sold varies based upon the objective of the campaign. TikTok’s availability page has two different country lists for Traffic. The first list includes Brazil, Australia, and all other countries’ advertisers who are trying to reach users in the U.S., and the second list includes Japan but not Brazil, Australia or the rest-of-world provision. Lead Generation campaigns are listed as available in the U.S. only. TopSearch campaigns are available to “Select accounts in supported markets,” and there are 20 countries listed for TopSearch.

Top 1 Impression Share is a related measure to Google’s Search absolute top impression share. The latter counts the number of impressions from “the very first ad among top ads” against the estimated number of impressions the advertiser would have been eligible for out of all the top ads. While both TikTok pages define the base of their metrics differently, “eligible search results impressions” according to the help page and “eligible search auctions” according to the preview, there are no publicly available resources showing the two platforms count the same way.

TikTok’s reporting guide says that keywords and search terms with low conversions “cannot be disclosed individually,” which “may cause a discrepancy in total conversions.” Keyword reporting is described as “a landscape report to provide insights on top-performing keywords” and full analysis, it says, should be done at the ad group and campaign levels, and not at the keyword level.

The keyword report provides you with the best-performing keywords, but may not reflect your campaigns’ total conversion numbers.

On the same reporting guide page it states that advertisers are unable to report on search terms or keywords broken out by creative (because of privacy). Data for iOS14 SKAN (Apple’s SKAdNetwork) for conversion, CPA, and conversion rate for Search Ads “are not currently available for the search ads campaign.” Brand safety and viewability measurement from third-party sources on the results page “may be subject to limitations.”

In addition, search campaign metrics lag behind by about eight hours. The guide says “Partial conversions will be available to advertisers after two days” and “Most conversions will display after three days.”

The automated terms report displays all automated terms as a single line item, with a drop-down menu showing the ten highest spending terms, the Automated Keywords page states. In addition, a search term that matches without an explicit keyword can display as “unknown.”

Neither the Impression Share help page nor the reporting guide indicates how to separate lost impression share by budget and by rank. On their help page, they list budget, bidding, keyword coverage, creative relevance, competition and changes in search demand as possible things to look at when assessing lost impression share; and sort the first two (budget and bidding) by if you spent your entire budget. Google’s page, by contrast, splits lost share into separate budget and rank metrics.

Reddit announced a test of ads in its search pages on Nov. 3 with a select group of eligible advertisers. The announcement says ads “may appear based on a range of factors, including contextual relevance to a user’s search query,” using Reddit’s existing targeting. It does not describe keyword controls.

In March 2023, Instagram announced ads in search results, showing “in the feed that people can scroll when they tap into a post from search results.” Both platforms announced a placement, while TikTok documented its keyword tools, a distinction for search teams to work with.

When you initially open the TikTok account, one of the first things you’ll recognize will be the search terms report. A manager who runs weekly Google search term reviews already has the expertise for that review, the negative keywords, and the ‘unknown’ rows.

The second item to verify when opening the TikTok account is whether the Automated Keywords toggle is turned on. The feature is enabled by default for SMB accounts, and additions arrive without notice.

Your campaign objective follows your business goal. According to TikTok’s optimization guide, if you run a Web Conversion campaign that cannot build volume, you can “temporarily switch to the Traffic objective to build volume,” and return to Web Conversion once the campaign is stable. During the Traffic phase, you can view match-type reporting and use keyword-level bids under a Cost Cap bid. Both of these disappear when you return to Web Conversion. However, keyword targeting (and its corresponding negatives), along with the search terms review, remain throughout.

Producing assets that match the keyword themes means working with whoever makes the company’s TikTok creative. Keyword totals show the top performers, TikTok points overall analysis to the ad group and campaign, and iOS14 SKAN conversion data is missing, so a cross-platform report needs a TikTok search row that says what it can and cannot show.

eMarketer’s U.S. search ad revenue forecast “excludes display-oriented formats on social networks, even if triggered by search queries,” and its social network ad spending forecast “includes all the various formats of advertising on those platforms.” That is how eMarketer draws its lines. This is useful context for anyone comparing TikTok search spend with its reported figures, though it doesn’t clarify where a company records the expense.

TikTok describes TopSearch as a visibility product. Its help center lists “brand, category, competitor, seasonal, or tentpole queries.”

As such, competitor queries listed as part of TikTok’s own lists constitute a brand-defense use case to assess, not a budget line to assume.

Paid search skills apply directly to TikTok’s keyword campaigns. The objective, automation defaults, creative requirements and reporting limits determine how far they carry. Budget ownership is still a company decision.

The campaigns for holiday promotions currently being run will be the ones described through the Impression Share columns in Ads Manager, on accounts where it shows them.

In addition, this quarter there will be a keyword-level read of spend on accounts that have the metrics. All accounts that have Automated Keywords enabled will see how those terms performed in campaign reporting and in the search terms report.

Reddit’s test is one to watch for anything more on search-specific keyword controls.

Why Micron Stock Bounced Back Friday

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At 5x forward earnings, Micron stock is too cheap to sell.

Micron (MU +7.01%) investors had a rough week, no doubt about it -- but things are getting better.

A major player in both DRAM and NAND flash memory production, Micron stock shed 26% through Thursday's close. As of 10:25 a.m. ET Friday morning, however, the stock is bouncing back 6.3%.

Election 2026 put Democrats in control of both the U.S. Senate and the House and installed Democratic governors in all four states where data center construction is most active. If investors are operating on the theory that Democrats as a whole are hostile to AI and data centers, then it makes sense to worry that data center construction will slow down, the sale of memory chips needed to build those data centers will slow also -- and Micron stock will fall.

But there's also a chance that none of this will happen -- or that if it does, it still won't hurt Micron that much.

After all, according to Citigroup, 2027 will see memory companies increase production by 22%, while demand grows by 26%, maintaining a supply-demand imbalance that should spur sales and keep memory prices high.

This morning, analyst KC Rajkumar at LYNX Equity Strategies seconded that sentiment, confirming that supply shortages, particularly in memory and flash markets, remain tight, and that Asian memory and flash markets aren't showing any drop-off in sales post-election -- at least not yet.

Granted, in the long term, there's a risk that the AI tide could turn against data centers, slowing Micron's growth. In the near term, however, at least through 2027, Micron's future looks secure. Trading for less than 5x the profit it's expected to earn in 2027, Micron looks almost too cheap not to buy.

I built AgentMon Live to keep track of Claude Code. Now it has a browser view.

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Running a few Claude Code sessions at once is great until I forget which one is doing what.

One is working through a change. Another has subagents running. A third might be waiting for me, but I won't know until I check that terminal again.

I kept bouncing between them to answer a few basic questions: What's happening? Does anything need me? Is the work actually finished?

It's a Claude Code mod that lets you watch Claude and its subagents work, see costs and usage limits, and spot when something needs your attention. It also checks for unfinished work before Claude says "done".

I shared an earlier release recently. This update adds the part I've been most excited about: you can now follow your agents live in your browser.

You keep working in Claude Code as usual. Open the browser view alongside it, and you can follow agent activity in real time and switch between sessions running in different terminals.

For my workflow, that last part matters a lot. Checking on several sessions means remembering where each one lives, switching terminals, and finding the relevant output. Having a browser view gives me somewhere to look while the work carries on.

The pane beside the chat is still available too. If you're working in one session, you might prefer keeping everything together. When several sessions are going, the browser gives you another way to follow them.

It's a simple addition to describe, but it's the one I've really wanted to use myself.

Seeing activity is useful. Knowing when to pay attention is more useful.

AgentMon brings together running tool calls, subagent activity, changed files, and verification results. It also shows costs and how close you are to your usage limits.

That helps with the questions I keep coming back to:

I want to be able to glance over, understand the situation, and get back to what I was doing.

The other part of AgentMon I care about is the completion check.

Consider a session where Claude runs the tests successfully, makes another code change, and then reports that everything passes. The earlier test result doesn't verify the latest edit.

AgentMon's "done gate" checks the recorded work before Claude finishes. It can catch things such as failed checks, tests that became stale after an edit, and unfinished todos. When it finds unfinished work, it sends that back to Claude once.

I still review the result myself. This helps catch some of the things I'd otherwise have to find by reading back through the session.

The README explains the checks in more detail.

In a Claude Code session, install it with:

Follow the marketplace and scope prompts. You can open the pane with:

It also works with Code inside Claude Desktop. For that setup, install AgentMon as a mod in Claude Desktop itself rather than through Claude Code in your terminal.

The project is open source, with installation instructions on GitHub.

If you tried an earlier release, I'd love to hear how the browser view fits into your workflow. I'm especially interested in hearing from people juggling several sessions.

What do you find yourself checking most often while your agents are working?

AI memory management: from 30 KB to 11 KB without losing facts

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at wallabytoken.com

The fact was on disk. Twice. Our first winback draft included a customer segment that our current records did not support. That was the moment we stopped treating this as a copy problem and started treating it as a memory architecture problem.

We found 30,195 bytes in our central L2 file: 28 completed items, 28 migration pointers, and a legacy error log. We rebuilt it into an 11,228-byte view with 33 facts intact. This is what changed, what we measured, and why deleting was not the point.

Disclosure: Wallaby provides inference-as-a-service for curated large language models. This is a field note from the memory system we use for our own work, not a claim about customer outcomes.

The relevant customer records were explicit: the proposed segment was not eligible for this campaign. Yet the first draft still had a "previously topped up, now inactive" segment. That segment did not match the people the message was meant to reach.

The correct state existed in two domain records. The draft had not used it. This was not merely stale copy. It was a retrieval and lifecycle problem: the right state was present, but the task that needed it had no reliable way to see it.

A better search engine might have found the right line. It would not have told us which line should still be visible, which domain owned it, or when it had expired.

The central file had grown to 179 lines and 30,195 bytes. The raw counts were useful because they showed that this was not simply a file with too many words.

The file was trying to be five things at once: a cross-domain baseline, a current work board, a domain-state copy, a history archive, and a routing index. It also held a sixth role, the old error register, which had its own proper home elsewhere.

This explained the bloat, but not the missed fact. For that, we had to look at the lifecycle.

Completed work was marked with a strike-through, not moved out of the current view. Migration to five domain READMEs had left behind repeated pointers. Our status checker still searched the old locations rather than all five domains. Dates told us when something had been written, not whether it remained true. And the old error summaries had never been formally retired.

The issue was not a lack of rules. The rules were spread across the system, but they did not form a usable page.

Our first temptation was to make retrieval smarter. That would have addressed the visible symptom and hidden the architecture problem.

We already had event logs, domain projections, an append-only write path, and a query command that returned JSON or one-line Markdown. We did not need another memory store, a vector index, or a new semantic query layer.

The central L2 became a shared current page for humans and AI, not a second task database and not a smaller copy of everything that had ever happened.

The implementation reused domain_state.py, domains.json, the existing event writer, and the existing tests. The only new pieces were a composition entry point and a curated list of sections.

That preview command assembled the anchors into readable Markdown. It did not write L2 or create a second source of truth. Missing anchors failed closed. valid_until exposed stale states. Closed items left the preview after one shift.

The first preview had three anchors: the revenue baseline, current pricing, and the current product homepage. That was enough to prove the mechanism. It was not enough to replace the page.

Next, we migrated the current focus, in-flight work, recently completed items, and account states. The expanded list covered 33 display anchors. The candidate and production files then matched byte for byte, which let us check the replacement instead of assuming it was faithful.

A few implementation details mattered more than the framework:

We wrote the acceptance criteria before calling the work complete. They were intentionally unglamorous.

The projection and freshness tests passed 35 of 35. The wider domain-state regression passed 51 of 51. The index tests passed 10 of 10, and the unified-write integration tests passed 5 of 5. After we fixed the false positive described below, the domain-state suite was 54 of 54.

These are not claims about memory accuracy or business results. They are claims about what the implementation and fixtures demonstrated.

One remaining drift warning would have been easy to misread as a migration failure. It pointed at or-application in the growth README.

The current state was fine. The warning came from an older activation log containing or-application as its anchor. Our legacy-anchor scanner was treating an audit trail as if it were a duplicate current state.

We changed the scanner to exclude proj:activation audit views from the legacy-anchor check. The check still fails on a genuine old anchor outside the projection. It just no longer mistakes an audit log for one.

That small fix produced drift=[] and brought the domain-state suite to 54 passing tests. More importantly, it kept the distinction between a current-state projection and an append-only audit record. Confusing those two would have recreated the original retrieval problem in a new form.

We have not measured a change in customer conversion, memory quality, or long-term maintenance cost. A smaller file is not automatically a better memory system. A passing test suite is not proof that the next real task will retrieve the right context every time.

The current evidence supports narrower conclusions. The central page is smaller. The facts we required are present. The preview updates from events. Closed items leave on schedule. Stale states fail visibly. The old write path still works, and the rollback file exists.

The next checks are equally concrete: whether new events update the central page without hand copies, whether closed items keep leaving on schedule, and whether a real task can answer the current baseline without opening unrelated domain files. If those checks fail, the response should be to inspect the curated list and read path, not to restore the old pile of narrative.

For us, the useful change is not less memory. It is less ambiguity about what is current, who owns it, and when it should stop being shown as current.

Our earlier posts cover the adjacent layers: why memory needs an audit between its log and current state, how we grouped 31 control points into 9 and compared shadow projections, and why a saved status is not evidence of a durable write. The broader upgrade and verification flow is in AI agent memory governance.

Originally published on the Wallaby Dispatch.

Two dead after bus crashes in France, officials say

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

French gendarmes and rescue workers at the scene of the crash in Lent, eastern France

A young girl and an 18-year-old man have died after a bus crashed in eastern France, local authorities have said.

The bus was travelling between Lyon and Bourg-en-Bresse when it crashed at around 07:00 local time on Friday in the town of Lent, about 34 miles (55km) from the border with Switzerland.

The bus was carrying 43 passengers, including 15 children, the Bourg-en-Bresse prosecutor's office said. Three people are in a critical condition and at least 13 others have sustained minor injuries.

French President Emmanuel Macron said his thoughts were with the victims, the injured, and their families.

In a post on X, he said the nation stood by their side and thanked the rescue teams involved.

The town's mayor Yves Cristin told French media the bus "left the road and hit a tree in the ditch".

The two people killed have been identified by authorities. However, there have been differing reports as to the age of the girl.

The prosecutor's office said it was "opening an investigation into the incident, on charges of road homicide and aggravated road traffic injuries".

The driver has been taken to hospital after sustaining injuries and has not yet been questioned by police. French Transport Minister Philippe Tabarot said initial tests indicated there were no traces of alcohol or drugs in his blood.

Photos circulating in French media show a blue and white bus tilted in what appears to be a ditch on the roadside. Trees line the side of the road. The front of the bus appears to be heavily damaged.

Local mayor Cristin told BFM TV: "I arrived minutes after the accident and people were in shock.

"The side of the bus was torn off, and clearly, the people who were on that side must have been seriously injured."

Addressing weather conditions at the time of the crash, the mayor told Le Figaro "there was a bit of fog, the road was wet" but there were "no major difficulties".

Speaking to the same paper, Fabrice Pannekoucke, head of the Auvergne-RhÃ´ne-Alpes region, said: "At this stage, there are no particular explanations or interpretations that can be given."

Tabarot said the bus did not operate "exclusively on school routes" but that there were "many schoolchildren on board".

Transport Minister Philippe Tabarot (centre) arrived at the scene following the crash

French Prime Minister SÃ©bastien Lecornu said the accident was tragic, and thanked the firefighters, medical teams and police who attended the scene.

Earlier, French Education Minister Ãdouard Geffray said it was "always difficult when our children are victims of accidents of this nature".

Emergency services were at the scene and medical helicopters were deployed to take victims with serious injuries to a specialised hospital.

More than 100 firefighters and 50 gendarmes were also at the scene.

The local town hall was opened to provide shelter and coordinate with emergency services, the local mayor said.

A recent Nintendo Indie World reveals tons of hidden gems on PC too

As 2026 winds down, we’re now getting to the stretch of the year where developers are showcasing what games are coming next year. If you’re a PC player who skipped over the show, you’ll be delighted to know that just about every game shown during it is also coming to Steam.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

After years of hype, the cozy Witchbrook is finally set to launch on Jan. 28. The multiplayer life sim drops you into a magical seaside town as you attend witch school. It’s shaping up to be a charming alternative to Harry Potter, where you and up to three other friends can live our your witchy days together.

City of None is a new 2D platformer from the co-creator of Celeste, and you shouldn’t need much more info than that to get excited about it. My Arms Are Longer Now has a one-of-a-king pitch, in that you control a very long arm that’s out to make everyone’s life miserable. While you wait for Square Enix to remake Chrono Trigger, developer Riyo Games is taking matters into its own hands. Threads of Time is an era-spanning turn-based RPG that is very clearly inspired by the classic RPG. Steeped in Japanese folklore, Kitsune: The Journey of Adashino is a 3D adventure game about a fox girl and her froggy friend exploring a strange town. Light is the key to survival, as you’ll use it to rekindle shrines and fight off monsters.

While it feels like we may never get another SSX game, Hyperyuki: Snowboarding Syndicate may very well be the next best thing. The fast-paced snowboarding game promises stylish fun as you shred rails and trick your way towards victory. If it can get the soundtrack right, it should scratch a very specific itch quite well.

As the CBS reality series continues on the 2026 TV schedule, Brady spoke to Parade about how his gameplay isn't that different than how he acted on the football field or with his fellow pro wrestlers. Michael Jordan said it in The Last Dance. I play to win. People called him a 'tyrant.' If you don’t want to play like me, don’t play like me. But that’s how I play, man. Brady doesn't feel the need to recant or apologize for his actions on Survivor, including screaming at teammates when they lost challenges. I’m Fascinated By One Survivor Castaway Who Is Getting An Almost Comically Negative Edit

This see-through camera lets photographers maintain eye contact with their subjects

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The HOA A1 incorporates a large viewfinder into its design so you’re not staring at a screen.﻿

The HOA A1 incorporates a large viewfinder into its design so you’re not staring at a screen.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Lutra, a South Korean startup, has debuted a new brand called Humans of Authenticity (HOA) that’s launching another screen-free digital camera that incorporates a large open viewfinder into its design, as spotted by PetaPixel. The camera is reminiscent of the Godox C100 but the HOA A1 positions its lens and sensor close to the center of its large viewfinder window so that a photographer can maintain eye contact with their subject, or feel more in the moment while framing a shot without staring at a screen.

The HOA A1 First Edition is expected to launch on December 1st in a very limited run of just 100 units. Preorders haven’t started yet, but the camera will be priced at 360,000 South Korean won, or $289 for US customers. The A1 features a 16-megapixel, 1/2.53-inch Sony sensor paired with a 26mm f/1.75 lens. There is a tiny LCD display on the back but it’s used to display information like battery life and your remaining shots. Everything else about the camera is strictly point and shoot with a shutter button located on its top edge.

The HOA website doesn’t include extensive details about the camera’s capabilities, or even sample shots captured by the A1. According to the site the company is still “fine-tuning the camera’s color and tone in collaboration with photographers.” It does confirm that instead of copying photos to a device or PC using a USB cable or by removing a memory card, the A1 connects to Wi-Fi and wirelessly makes your shots available by tapping your smartphone to the back of the camera.

A wireless connection to the internet isn’t needed to take photos, but you’ll need access to Wi-Fi to view and download your shots. The A1 uses a system called ShootProof that authenticates every image captured with an anonymous certificate recorded to a public blockchain. That feature doesn’t appear optional, but there’s also a signature mode you can turn on or off that lets you sign your name in an accompanying app and then have it overlaid in the corner of every photo.

Formulated with hyaluronic acid and hydrolyzed collagen, the high shine gloss leaves my lips feeling deeply hydrated and super plumped. The color payoff is strong enough that I can skip lipstick and wear it with just a stain or lip liner underneath.

Stuff their stocking with a real winter essential: hand cream. L’Occitane’s Holiday Hand Cream Trio comes with three 1-ounce tubes that can live in their bag. Made from shea butter, the formula delivers long-lasting hydration without feeling too thick and slippery. The nourishing hand creams come in three festive scents: original shea, praline, and pine.

A set of fun press-on nails will always come in handy when they need an emergency mani. Get them this gorgeous cat-eye style that’s perfect for New Year’s Eve, or snag them other sets Glamnetic sells. Our editors can attest that they last for weeks when applied correctly, so you’re basically gifting them a free salon-level manicure.

When winter has me dreaming of warmer days, Sol de Janeiro’s tropical-scented bodycare set gives my shower routine a mini vacation. With notes of vanilla orchid and sheer sandalwood, this travel-size set includes Delícia Drench Shower Oil for leaving skin silky-soft post-shower, the rich Delícia Drench Body Butter, which helps lock in moisture, and the Cheirosa 59 Perfume Mist to match.

A Cosmo Holy Grail Beauty Award winner, Cyklar’s Perfume Oil is the perfect purse-sized pick for a quick scent refresh on the go. “The scent is so warm, rich, and creamy without being sickly sweet or smelling artificial,” says senior director of editorial operations, Danielle Colin-Thome. “I couldn’t wait to finish testing the other perfume oils on my list so I could go back to using this every day.”

Perfect for the jet-setter in your life, this little set of skincare patches will help revive their complexion, especially after a long travel day. It comes with two hydrating sheet masks infused with hyaluronic acid and vitamin B5, two pairs of under-eye patches (one for daytime and one for nighttime), and a lip mask.

If a Rhode Pocket Blush showed up in my stocking, I’d be doing a very happy little dance. So if you’re shopping for a fellow Rhode fan, it’s never a bad idea to restock their favorite shade. This blush is famous for its creamy formula that’s effortless to blend out for a natural-looking flush.

Winter dryness has me reaching for this hydrating mist on repeat, making it a useful addition to their stocking. It’s formulated with PDRN oil capsules, hyaluronic acid, and hydrolyzed collagen that leaves a light, even layer of moisture in just a few spritzes. My skin looks naturally dewy with or without makeup.

Between the delicious scents and plush, whipped texture, this body butter has our editors hooked.

“There’s something magical about this body butter...It beautifully melts into skin and feels weightless,” says contributor Mary Honkus. “The thickness is just right, and the scents are to die for.” It comes with three travel-size pouches so you can tuck one into a stocking or split them among your besties.

Okay, okay. The price on this one isn’t *exactly* stocking stuffer-friendly—but for the skincare-obsessed person on your list, the Depuffi is a little luxury that is totally worth the splurge. It offers hot and cool therapy with six temperature settings to help soothe facial tension and de-puff. “The shape fits the face perfectly, really getting in on all the contours and curves of my cheeks and jawline,” says assistant beauty editor Jasmine Hyman in her review. “The cooling setting is perfect to close out the facial by reducing post-suction redness and providing some extra depuffing.”

A Cosmo Readers’ Choice Award winner, Salt & Stone’s Body Wash Discovery Set is another easy gift to split among a few stockings. The luxe scents in this set—like saffron and cedar or bergamot and hinoki—smell way more elevated than their boring body washes. Or gift the full set to the friend who treats a long, steamy shower like their favorite way to unwind.

A girl can never have too many hair ties, and this set of mini silk scrunchies from Squigs is a useful stocking stuffer that will surely be appreciated. They have a soft, secure hold that will keep your hair out of the way without creasing or tugging. A little birdie told us that Gracie Abrams uses them too.

Hand sanitizer is a cold and flu season essential, but Gemi’s formula feels a touch more elevated than the usual alcohol-scented, ultra-drying kind. It comes in luxe scents like lavender and geranium, green tea and bergamot, and Fuji apple and rose, and it’s formulated with cica, vitamin B5, and green tea to help keep hands feeling hydrated. Get the version with an attached keychain, and it doubles as a cute bag charm.

When my skin needs a reset, I reach for Dr. Dennis Gross Skincare’s Alpha Beta Universal Daily Peel Pads. The two-step treatment helps smooth texture, brighten my complexion, and fade the look of dark spots over time. This set of five is the perfect little pick-me-up for dull winter skin, especially when my skin is craving a deeper exfoliation.

Striker Bojan Radulovic, who has missed the past four games with a hamstring problem, may be involved.

Captain Ryan Ledson is back from a three-game suspension after being found guilty by the FA of misconduct and he is in contention.

The former Preston player was sidelined with a shoulder problem prior to his ban and has not been involved at first-team level since the League One game at Mansfield Town on September 12.

Drury said: "There's only probably one (fresh) concern.

"It's Ilias Bronkhorst, who's just got a slight niggle in his hip. So, if he doesn't play tomorrow, that would be precautionary than anything else. In terms of everybody else from Tuesday night (against Luton), everybody came through fine.

"Obviously, Ryan Ledson's suspension has finished, so he'll be back available. Bojan has trained. If he's not involved tomorrow, he will be involved on Tuesday (in the EFL Trophy against Liverpool under-21s). He's pushing.

On whether he will make significant changes, Drury continued: "I wouldn't say significant. I think we'll make some changes. But, again, it's a competition to progress in and to do well in.

"There's an opportunity for some players not to play and an opportunity for other players to play. But at the same time, I won't be doing it just to hand game time out to people, that's not the way we work."

Airbnb can invest in Brian Chesky’s AI lab, its quarterly filing shows

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Airbnb’s technology chief holds a stake in Chesky’s AI lab, and independent directors approved the deal. It landed with Airbnb’s fastest revenue growth in three years and a 2027 date for its own AI agent.

Brian Chesky at an Airbnb event in San Francisco in 2018

Brian Chesky’s AI lab paid Airbnb $7.1M for some of its intellectual property and to cover certain expenses, Airbnb disclosed in its quarterly filing on Thursday. Chesky, Airbnb’s chief executive, founded the lab and controls it.

Under the deal, signed this month, Airbnb can choose to join the lab’s future funding rounds. It also gets a licence to use some of the lab’s technology inside its own business. Chief technology officer Ahmad Al-Dahle holds a stake in the lab and works for it too. The Information, which reported the disclosure, said Chesky has funded the lab himself so far, citing a source. TNW has not independently verified that.

“Their roles with the AI Lab do not change their duties and responsibilities to Airbnb,” the filing said of the two executives.

A special committee of independent board members approved the deal under Airbnb’s policy on related-party transactions. The filing does not name the lab or say what it is building.

Al-Dahle joined Airbnb in January from Meta, where he led generative AI, Bloomberg reported at the time. We reported in June that Chesky was building an AI lab of his own.

The filing came with Airbnb’s third-quarter results. Revenue rose 19% to $4.9bn, its fastest growth in more than three years, and net income reached $1.9bn. Nights and seats booked rose 16%. Airbnb now expects revenue to grow in the high teens this year, up from at least the mid-teens. It also approved up to $12bn more in share buybacks.

“It’s rare for a marketplace of our size to reaccelerate growth, but that’s exactly what we’re doing,” Chesky told analysts on Thursday’s earnings call.

Airbnb last raised its forecast in August. Hotels, which it began adding this year, grew “markedly faster” than homes in the quarter, Chesky said.

Shares jumped more than 10% on Friday, Reuters reported. That set Airbnb apart from Booking, Expedia and Tripadvisor. All three have pointed to higher airfares linked to the Iran war and to unrest in parts of Europe, Reuters said.

Airbnb plans to start rolling out its own AI agent for planning and booking trips in the first half of 2027, it said alongside the results. Instead of a chat window, the agent will build each answer from maps, photos, comparisons, calendars and itineraries. It will also handle changes, refunds and payment problems after a booking.

Chesky argued that general assistants such as Meta’s Muse and Instinct are not built for travel. They still send Airbnb little business, he said on the call, as Business Insider reported.

“We’re not seeing a lot of traffic, and even fewer bookings, from Muse and Instinct yet,” Chesky said.

Agent traffic peaked in early October and has not grown since, he added. Airbnb’s own support assistant now resolves about 40% of customer issues without a human. Support costs per booking fell more than 17% from a year earlier, the company said.

ScreenRant And Polygon Owner Valnet Agrees To $2 Million Settlement In Class Action Lawsuit

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Digital MediaGame JournalismGames MediaValnet

A few years back, former Valnet freelancer Daniel Quintiliano filed a class action lawsuit against the media company accusing it of misclassifying content makers across its many websites as independent contractors rather than full-time employees in order to deny them certain levels of pay and benefits. Valnet denied the charges, but recently agreed to a $2 million settlement that will pay out to affected contractors.

As reported by Vertical Slice, Quintiliano accused Valnet of failing to properly compensate writers for things like overtime work and business expenses, and even failed to pay minimum wage in some cases. “By signing a settlement agreement and agreeing to ask the Court to enter a judgment ending the Action, Plaintiff and Valnet have negotiated a proposed settlement that is subject to the Courtâs final approval. Both sides agree the proposed settlement is a compromise of disputed claims,” reads an announcement on the settlement website by administrator Atticus. “By agreeing to settle, Valnet does not admit any violations or concede the merit of any claims.”

The proposed $2 million will cover all all contractors involved in written and edited articles and video content between December 2019 and August 2026, provided they gave Valnet a California home address while they were classified as an independent contractor for one of its websites. The settlement has been granted preliminary approval and will get a final sign-off in February before the payments are given out.

Valnet began building its media empire in 2016 with the acquisition of Comic Book Resources. It later obtained notable brands like Collider and Polygon, for which it reportedly paid nearly $20 million. Polygon‘s masthead was gutted in the process and the bulk of what it publishes now comes from freelancers. There has been ongoing turnover at some of Valnet’s other websites as well, including TheGamer, which has recently seen several rounds of departures.
